Order filed by Chief Judge Juan R. 
Torruella, Judge Hugh H. Bownes, 
Judge Kermit V. Lipez. The State of 
Washington’s motion for 
reconsideration of our order denying it 
and other states leave to file an amici 
brief is denied. Although we value the 
input of the various States, accepting 
their brief in this case would create a 
recusal issue for one of the judges on 
the panel. In keeping with circuit 
policy, we will not accept an amici brief 
under these circumstances. So Ordered.

COMPLAINT 
*x* * * * 


2. ...Although the stated goal of the Challenged 
Regulations is to reduce the incidence of underage tobacco use, 
the purpose and effect of advertising brands of cigarettes is not 
to persuade non-smokers to begin to smoke, but rather to alter 
or reinforce the brand preferences of smokers. 


** * * 


15. On-site advertising of tobacco products also 
constitutes an important means by which convenience stores, 
gas stations and other retail stores that sell tobacco products 
generate sales, not only of tobacco products but also of other 
items on sale. To stimulate traffic in such stores, it is important 
to communicate to adult smokers that they can purchase 


a 


15 


popular brands of cigarettes inside the store. Advertisements 
for tobacco products often trigger the kind of spontaneous 
purchase decisions that get customers inside the store. 


* * kK * 


Respectfully submitted, 


OF COUNSEL: PHILIP MORRIS

COMPLAINT FOR DECLARATORY 
AND INJUNCTIVE RELIEF 


re of Action 


1. This is an action to declare null and void, and to enjoin 
enforcement of, 940 CMR 21.04(1)(a), (1)(d), 21.04(2)(a), 
(2)(c), (2)(d), 21.04(5) and 21.04(6) (the “Advertising and 
Marketing Regulations” or the “Regulations” attached as 
Exhibit A hereto) promulgated by the Attorney General of the 
Commonwealth of Massachusetts purportedly under Mass. 
Gen. Laws ch. 93A, § 2(c). The Advertising and Marketing 
Regulations (1) prohibit constitutionally protected advertising 
and promotion practices by manufacturers of smokeless 
tobacco products, in part, by categorically denominating such 
lawful activities as purportedly “unfair or deceptive” acts or 
practices, and (2) unconstitutionally restrict protected retail 
practices regarding the lawful promotion of smokeless tobacco 
products to adults. 


17 


ieee 


2. The Advertising and Marketing Regulations would ban 
the following means of lawful advertising of smokeless tobacco 
products without regard to whether any particular 
advertisement is “unfair or deceptive”: 


(a) Outdoor advertising, including advertising in 
enclosed stadiums and advertising within a retail establishment 
that is directed toward or visible from the outside of the 
establishment, in any location that is within a one thousand foot 
radius of any public playground, playground area in a public 
park, elementary school or secondary school. § 21 .04(5)(a); 
and 


(b) Point-of-sale advertising of cigarettes or smokeless 
tobacco products any portion of which is placed lower than 
five feet from the floor of any retail establishment accessible to 
persons younger than 18 years old, which is located within a 
one thousand foot radius of any public play-ground, 
playground area in a public park, elementary school or 
secondary school. § 21 .04(5)(b). 


3. The following sign, however, is permitted: 


(a) A retailer may place one sign no larger than 576 
Square inches and containing only black text on a white 
background stating “Tobacco Products Sold Here” on the 
outside or visible from the outside of each location where such 
products are offered for sale. § 21.04(6). 


Promotional Restrictions 
4. The Advertising and Marketing Regulations would ban 
the following retail practices, among others: 


(a) Sampling, promotional giveaways, or any other free 
distribution of cigarettes or smokeless tobacco products. 


§ 21.04(1)(a); 


18 


(b) Sale or distribution of cigarettes or smokeless 
tobacco products if any gift or item other than cigarettes or 
smokeless tobacco is offered in consideration of the purchase. 
§ 21.04(1)(d). 


Retail Outlet Sales Restricti 


5. The Advertising and Marketing Regulations would ban 
the following retail outlet sales practices, among others: 


(a) Selling cigarettes or smokeless tobacco products in 
any manner other than in a direct, face-to-face exchange 
without the assistance of any vending machines or any other 
electronic or mechanical device. § 21.04(2)(a); 


(b) Using self-service displays of cigarettes or 
smokeless tobacco products. § 21.04(2)(c); and 


(c) Failing to place cigarettes and smokeless tobacco 
products out of the reach of all consumers, and in a location 
accessible only to outlet personnel. § 21.04(2)(d). 

N FClai 

6. The Advertising and Marketing Regulations 
promulgated by the Attorney General violate the First and 
Fourteenth Amendments of the United States Constitution by 
abridging plaintiff's right to free commercial speech. By 
purporting to directly regulate interstate commerce outside of 
Massachusetts, the Advertising and Marketing Regulations also 
violate the Commerce Clause, Article I, Section 8, Clause 3 of 
the Constitution. Moreover, as applied to adult-only 
establishments, certain Regulations are irrational in violation of 
the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment to 
the Constitution.

Facts Applicable to All Counts 


11. USTC employs various methods of advertising and 

promoting its products in Massachusetts, including certain 
advertising, self-service displays and the placement of 
promotional materials in retail Stores. USTC’s marketing 
activities also include offering samples and promotional 
give-aways in adult-only facilities. 


12. The Advertising and Marketing Regulations are an 
unconstitutional encroachment on plaintiffs ability to lawfully 
advertise and promote its smokeless tobacco products to 
adults. The requirements of the Regulations establish an actual 
and justiciable controversy between plaintiff and defendant as 


20 


to whether the Regulations violate the United States 
Constitution. 


13. Compliance with the terms of the Regulations would 
cause plaintiff irreparable injury, including the abridgement of 
plaintiffs constitutional right to free commercial speech. 


14. The Attorney General’s Advertising and Marketing 
Regulations would prohibit the plaintiff from lawfully engaging 
in constitutionally protected advertising and promotional 
activities in violation of the First and Fourteenth Amendments 
to the United States Constitution. The practical effect of the 
Regulations is virtually to ban the lawful advertising of 
smokeless tobacco products in Massachusetts (except in the 
most rural areas) under the guise of preventing deceptive or 
unfair trade practices. 


15. The Advertising and Marketing Regulations violate the 
First Amendment of the Constitution by abridging plaintiff s 
night to free commercial speech. Advertising directed toward 
the lawful promotion of a legal product cannot unilaterally be 
prohibited by the Attorney General because advertising that 
does not directly incite illicit activity is protected commercial 
speech. The Attorney General’s asserted interest in curbing 
purportedly deceptive or unfair advertising is not substantial 
enough to justify banning lawful smokeless tobacco advertising 
directed at adults. Nor do the Regulations materially advance 
the asserted goal. The Regulations are also not sufficiently 
narrowly tailored to ensure that they are no more extensive 
than necessary to advance the interests asserted by the 
Attorney General. Moreover, the only exception to the 
Attorney General’s blanket rule is for a sign no larger than four 
square feet “containing only black text on a white background 
stating ‘Tobacco Products Sold Here.” That paltry exception 
is plainly insufficient to cure the constitutional deficiencies of 
the Regulations. 


2] 


16. Sections 21.04(1)(a), (1)(d), 21.04(2)(a), (2)(c), 
(2)(d), 21.04(5) and 21.04(6) of the Regulations would, in 
effect, ban constitutionally protected advertising and marketing 
practices in the Commonwealth. The Attorney General cannot 
constitutionally impose such a pervasive prohibition where 
truthful, nonmisleading advertising will be abridged along with 
allegedly deceptive or unfair commercial speech. The 
protected speech contained within the plaintifP's advertising 
relates to an activity that is lawful for adults. The Attorney 
General cannot by unilateral action prohibit constitutionally 
protected advertising and promotional practices where less 
restrictive means are available to adequately achieve his 
Purported goals. Because the Regulations are unjustifiably 
more extensive than necessary to serve the interests they 
ostensibly were designed to protect, and if enforced would 
impose unwarranted economic harm to the plaintiff engaged in 
the lawful activity of selling smokeless tobacco products to 
adults, they are unconstitutional under the First Amendment. 


17. The plaintiff's brands of smokeless tobacco products, 
which are manufactured in other states, have been distributed 
in interstate commerce for many years continuously to the 
present. Sections 21.04( 1)(a), (1)(d), 21.04(2)(a), (2)(c), and 
(2)(d) of the Regulations prohibit (i) the distribution of free 
samples; (ii) the distribution of non-tobacco gifts; (iii) selling 
smokeless products in any manner other than a direct 
face-to-face exchange; (iv) using self-service displays of 
smokeless tobacco products: and (v) failing to place smokeless 
tobacco products out of the reach of all customers, and in a 
location accessible only to outlet personnel. These Regulations 
are not limited on their face to Massachusetts. Since these 
restrictions are not limited to activities occurring in 
Massachusetts, they purport to directly regulate interstate 
commerce in violation of the Commerce Clause, Article I, 
Section 8, Clause 3 of the Constitution. 


22 


18. Sections 21.04(1)(a), (1)(d), 21.04(2)(a), (2)(c) and 
(2)(d), which (i) ban sampling and other free distribution, (ii) 
ban gift items in consideration of the purchase of smokeless 
tobacco products, (iii) permit only direct face-to-face sales, (iv) 
ban self-service displays, and (v) require that smokeless 
tobacco products be kept out of the reach of consumers, are 
irrational as applied to adult-only establishments. The lack of 
nexus between these sections and their asserted goal is further 
exacerbated by an internal inconsistency within the 
Regulations: the point-of-sale restriction expressly limits that 
provision to “retail establishment(s) accessible to persons 
younger than 18 years old.” § 21.04(5)(b). Unlike section 
21.04(5)(b), sections 21.04(1)(a), (1)(d), 21.04(2)(a), (2)(c) 
and (2)(d) are not limited to establishments only accessible to 
minors. As applied to adult-only establishments, they are not 
even rationally related to the Attorney General’s asserted 
purpose, and therefore violate the Equal Protection Clause of 
the Fourteenth Amendment. 


19. The Attorney General is a person within the meaning 
of 42 U S.C. § 1983 because plaintiff seeks to enjoin him from 
taking actions in his official capacity. In promulgating the 
Advertising and Marketing Regulations, ostensibly pursuant to 
the statutory authority of Mass. Gen. Laws ch. 93A, § 2, the 
Attorney General acted under color of state law. Enforcement 
of the Regulations would deprive plaintiff of its rights 
protected by federal law.

AMENDED COMPLAINT 
Introduction 


1. This is an action to declare null and void, and to enjoin 
enforcement 940 CMR. §§ 21.04(I)(a), ( 1 \(c), (1)(d), 
21.04(2)(c), (2)(d), 21.04(5) and 21.04(6) (the “Challenged 
Regulations”), promulgated by Defendant, the Attorney 
General of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. The 
Challenged Regulations impose unconstitutional restrictions on 
the ability of the Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corporation 
(“Brown & Williamson”) to advertise and promote its products 
in the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. 


2. Although the stated goal of the Challenged Regulations 
is to reduce the incidence of underage tobacco use, Defendant 
has not demonstrated and cannot demonstrate, as the First 
Amendment requires, that the Challenged Regulations will have 
the intended effect of. or are necessary to achieve, the stated 


4 


I 


28 


goal. It is clear, however, that the Challenged Regulations wi// 
limit severely Plaintiff's ability to communicate with adults 
about the products they sell as part of their legitimate 
competition for the adult consumer market. The restrictions 
are doubly onerous because Plaintiff's speech already is limited 
in other ways. The Commonwealth has ample alternative 
means available to it—including requirements and prohibitions 
set forth in state statutes and in other regulations promulgated 
by Defendant that have not been challenged in this action—to 
address minors’ access to tobacco products and the incidence 
of underage tobacco use while imposing a significantly lesser 
burden on Plaintiff's ability to communicate with adult 
consumers concerning tobacco products. Moreover, the 
Challenged Regulations operate categorically, deeming all 
covered activities as “unfair or deceptive” under Mass. Gen. 
Laws c.93A without regard to whether any particular 
advertisement or promotion is “unfair or deceptive” or 
otherwise proscribable. As a result, the Challenged 
Regulations violate Brown & Williamson’s right to freedom of 
speech secured by the First and Fourteenth Amendments of the 
United States Constitution. 


3. Sections 21.04(1)(a), (1)(c), (I)(d), 21.04(5), and 
21.04(6) of the Challenged Regulations also violate the 
Supremacy Clause of the U.S. Constitution by imposing 
requirements and prohibitions based on smoking and health 
with respect to the advertising or promotion of cigarettes that 
are labeled in accordance with the Federal Cigarette Labeling 
and Advertising Act, 15 U.S.C. § 1331 ef seg. Under 15 
U.S.C. § 1334(b), these Challenged Regulations are expressly 
preempted. 


4. In addition, certain of the Challenged Regulations ban 
various retail sales practices and forms of communication in all 
retail outlets. These practices and communications are even 
banned in those outlets that are not accessible to individuals 
under the age of 18 years, despite the rules’ alleged purpose 


29 


“to address the incidence of cigarette smoking ... by children 
under legal age,” and notwithstanding the fact that such venues 
are exempted from other provisions of the Challenged 
Regulations. To the extent that these portions of the 
Challenged Rules apply even in stores and other venues that 
are not accessible to individuals under 18, the Challenged Rules 
are not rationally related to their declared purpose, do not 
rationally further any legitimate state interest in preventing 
“unfair or deceptive” acts or practices, and therefore also 
violate the Equal Protection and Due Process provisions of the 
Fourteenth Amendment of the United States Constitution, as 
well as the tailoring requirement of the First and Fourteenth 
Amendments. 


5. To the extent that such regulations purport to prohibit 
transactions between persons outside of Massachusetts or that 
Otherwise lack a sufficient nexus to Massachusetts, the 
Challenged Regulations constitute impermissible extraterritorial 
regulation in violation of the Commerce Clause and the Due 
Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment. 


6. The implementation of the Challenged Regulations will 
give rise to irreparable global harm to Brown & Williamson, 
which faces the denial of its free speech and due process rights 
and the severe restriction of its ability to compete for retail 
sales with its competitors, with consequent lost sales the 
magnitude of which could not adequately be determined. 
Implementation of the Challenged Regulations will also give 
rise to irreparable harm to adult consumers of tobacco 
products, who will be denied lawful communications regarding 
products they may wish to purchase. 


7. The Challenged Regulations are unconstitutional and 
invalid. They should be preliminarily and permanently 
enjoined. 


30 


Parties 


8. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corporation is a 
Delaware corporation with its principal place of business in 
Kentucky. Brown & Williamson manufacturers cigarettes and 
smokeless tobacco products that are advertised, marketed and 
sold throughout the United States, including Massachusetts. 


9. The defendant, Thomas F. Reilly, is the Attorney 
General of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. Defendant’s 
predecessor promulgated the Challenged Regulations, 
purportedly pursuant to power granted to the Attorney General 
under Mass. Gen. Laws c.93A, § 2(c). 


furisdicti LV 
10. This Court has subject matter jurisdiction over this 
action by virtue of 28 U.S.C. §§ 1331 & 1343. Declaratory 


and injunctive relief are authorized by 28 U.S.C. §§ 2201-02 
and Fed. R. Civ. Proc. 57 and 65. 


11. Venue is proper in the district pursuant to 28 
U.S.C. § 1391(b). 


l 1 Clai 


12. Brown & Williamson is a non-Massachusetts 
corporation that manufactures cigarettes and smokeless 
tobacco products that are marketed and sold in Massachusetts. 
Brown & Williamson engages in advertising and promotional 
activities that are intended to promote sales of its brands to 
adult smokers. Historically, Brown & Williamson—like 
manufacturers of other consumer products—has employed a 
broad range of methods to advertise and promote its products, 
including print and broadcast advertising, billboards, coupons, 
various forms of price offers (e.g. “two for one” or “cents off’ 
promotions), brand sponsorship of athletic and cultural events, 
and placement of posters, decals, self-service displays and 
other promotional materials at or inside retail stores. Typically, 
these retail store promotions are pursuant to arrangements that 


3] 


involve financial incentives offered by Brown & Williamson (or 
by other manufacturers) to retailers in exchange for the display, 
at points of sale, of such materials. 


13. In recent years, however, Brown and Williamson’s 
speech has been progressively curtailed by law. Brown & 
Williamson has not advertised cigarettes on radio and television 
since Congress enacted 15 USC § 1335, a statute Brown & 
Williamson has never challenged. 


14. In November 1998, Brown & Williamson (and 
other tobacco companies), faced with lawsuits from various 
states seeking billions of dollars, entered into an agreement 
with the Attorneys General of 46 states, including 
Massachusetts, that resulted in further restrictions on the 
promotion and advertising of Cigareties (the “Agreement”). 
The terms of the Agreement required that Brown & 
Williamson, among other things, discontinue altogether most 
forms of outdoor or publicly visible cigarette advertising, 
including such advertising on billboards. This prohibition, like 
many others-in the Agreement, is incorporated into a judicially 
enforceable Consent Decree. 


15. The Agreement allows many forms of advertising 
displayed on the property of retail establishments, whether such 
advertising is located indoors or outside. Such on-site brand 
advertising (which is subject to a size limitation and other 
restrictions under the Agreement) constitutes one of the few 
remaining means for Brown & Williamson to communicate 
with adult smokers. The retail store environment is the primary 
place where consumers develop awareness of advertising and 
brand differences among competing cigarette products. That 
awareness is developed through the use of signage both inside 
and outside of the store and through the use of various product 
displays. Such advertising serves to supply consumers with 
pertinent information such as price and other brand attributes, 
to foster brand loyalty and to encourage smokers of other 
brands to try or switch to the advertised brands. 


32 


16. On-site advertising of tobacco products also 
constitutes an important means by which convenience stores, 
gas stations, and other small retail stores that sell tobacco 
products generate sales, not only of tobacco products but also 
of other items on sale. To stimulate traffic in such stores, it is 
important to communicate to adult consumers that they can 
purchase popular brands of cigarettes inside the store. 
Outdoor advertisements for tobacco products often trigger the 
kind of spontaneous purchase decisions that get customers 
inside the store. Once inside, these customers tend to make 
purchases of other products in addition to tobacco. 


17. Self-service displays of cigarettes provide price and 
other brand information that is crucial to the consumers’ choice 
between products. Importantly, they provide such information 
at a time and in a manner that the consumer can directly 
examine the product, review its label, and make an immediate 
purchase decision. 


18. Controlled-service displays are not adequate 
substitute for self-service displays. Controlled-service inhibits 
direct access to and comparison of products. They also 
separate perception of the message from the point-of-purchase 
decision. As a practical matter, controlled-service displays in 
most retail establishments must be immediately accessible to 
the cashier. In many establishments, such accessible space is 
extremely limited, many products compete for that space, and 
it contains a clutter of messages that interferes with effective 
communication about any given product. The limited space 
available is often devoted primarily or exclusively to brands 
with a significant market share, frequently in accordance with 
contracts between the retailer and competing manufacturers. 
The restrictions in the Challenged Regulations on advertising 
tobacco products at levels below five feet further restrict the 
available avenues of communication, and increase the potential 
that such limited areas will be dominated by the most popular 
brands that can-offer the best deals to retailers. Self-service 


EE EE 
33 


display are therefore vital in order to communicate with 
consumers regarding new brands and promotions or brands 
that appeal to a smaller share of the market. Because Brown 
& Williamson manufactures a wide variety or brand styles that 
do not tend to dominate a particular market, Brown & 
Williamson is particularly impacted by the ban on this means of 
communication. Other means of communication about tobacco 
products are sharply limited by other laws and, in any event, 
are not adequate. 


19. On January 22, 1999. Defendant promulgated 
regulations severely limiting the advertising and promotion of 
cigarettes and smokeless tobacco products, including the 
Challenged Regulations, purportedly pursuant to the Attorney 
General’s rule-making powers under Mass. Gen. Laws. c.93A, 
§ 2(c). (A copy of these regulations is attached to this 
Complaint as Attachment A.) Sections 2] .04(2)(c) and (2)(d) 
ban all self-service displays of tobacco products in all retail 
outlets, regardless of whether minors have physical or visual 
access to them. Sections 21.04(5) and (6) of the Challenged 
Regulations prohibit virtually all on-site outdoor advertising of 
tobacco products and any indoor advertising “directed toward” 
or “visible from” the outside of any retail establishment that is 
located within 1000 feet of any “public playground or 
playground area in a public park, elementary school, or 
secondary school.” Within such establishments, no cigarette 
advertising is permitted to be displayed less than five feet from 
the floor if the establishment is accessible to minors. The only 
publicly visible advertising that is permitted is a sign no larger 

‘than four square feet “containing only black text on a white 
background stated ‘Tobacco Products Sold Here’.” 


20. The effect of those prohibitions, individually and 
cumulatively, will be to ban most on-site advertising and 
promotion of tobacco products in Massachusetts, except in the 
most rural areas. The Challenged Regulations will virtually 
eliminate the already-limited space available for communication 


34 


with adult customers at the primary venue for such 
communication - Massachusetts retailers legally selling tobacco 
products to adult customers. 


21. In addition to its on-site advertising practices, 
Brown & Williamson has implemented various marketing and 
promotional programs intended to enhance the loyalty of adult 
smokers to particular brands. These programs play a 
significant role in the efforts of Brown & Williamson to 
compete with other manufacturers of cigarettes for market 
share. These programs can involve (subject to the limitations 
set forth in the Agreement) such common consumer marketing 
practices as the distribution of tobacco products by mail in 
redemption of coupons or proofs of purchase, distribution of 
product samples in adult-only establishments, distribution of 
promotional items attached to packs of cigarettes, and the 
offering of non-tobacco merchandise to adult customers who 
submit specified proofs of purchase. These programs typically 
operate nationwide and are not limited, or directed principally, 
to Massachusetts consumers. 


22. Sections 21.04(1)(a), (c) and (d) of the Challenged 
Regulations would severely limit these programs by prohibiting 
any manufacturer of any cigarettes sold in the Commonwealth 
from engaging in a variety of promotional activities, including 
promotional give-aways, distribution of cigarettes through the 
mail, and distribution of tobacco products if any gift or item 
other than cigarettes or smokeless tobacco is offered in 
consideration of the purchase. These prohibitions on their face 
are not limited to promotional activities occurring in 
Massachusetts or involving Massachusetts consumers. Rather, 
they purport to restrict such activities by the Plaintiff anywhere. 


23. The stated purpose of the Challenged Regulations 
is to restrict the advertising of tobacco products “in order to 
address the incidence of cigarette smoking and smokeless 
tobacco use by children under legal age.” Brown & 
Williamson does not market its products to minors. It limits 


35 


participation in promotional Programs to adult smokers and it 
has initiated and supported efforts to discourage underage 
smoking and sales to minors. In a well-established, mature 
product market, such as the market for tobacco products, the 
principal function of advertising and promotion is to either 
reinforce or change the brand preferences of those who have 
already made the decision to use the product. Accordingly, the 
Commonwealth is unlikely to be able to demonstrate that the 
Challenged Regulations will advance the stated goal of the 
Commonwealth to any significant degree. 


24. There are many steps the Commonwealth and its 
political subdivisions can take to prevent or discourage 
smoking by minors. It is illegal in Massachusetts for any 
person to sell tobacco products to minors or for any person 
(other than a parent or guardian) to give tobacco products to 
minors. Mass. Gen. Laws ¢.270, § 6. The vigorous 
enforcement of such laws will address the need to reduce youth 
smoking without interfering with Plaintiff's legitimate 
commercial speech. 


25. Moreover, in contrast to many other jurisdictions, 
Massachusetts has no general law imposing penalties on minors 
for possession or use of tobacco products. A credible risk of 
incurring a significant monetary or other penalty (such as 
suspension of a driver’s license, e.g., 7 Vermont Stat. Ann. § 
1005S) will address the need to reduce youth smoking without 
interfering with Plaintiffs legitimate commercial speech. 


26. The Commonwealth and its political subdivisions 
can also address underage smoking by means of educational 
programs. The Commonwealth has long touted the 
effectiveness of its antitobacco media campaigns. The recent 
settlement may result in the Payment to Massachusetts of more 
than 57 billion over a period of years, funds that can be used to 
create additional anti-smoking programs. 


36 


27. The Attorney General’s Office in particular has 
recognized the effectiveness of these alternatives to restricting 
the advertising of tobacco products to adult consumers. At a 
press conference at which he announced his decision to agree 
to the national settlement on behalf of the Commonwealth, 
Defendant’s predecessor publicly announced the “Harshbarger 
Health Plan,” which proposed to use the funds from the 
settlement to “reduce tobacco use among children and adults 
... through tobacco control programs, school-based programs, 
research and evaluation, and cessation-on-demand programs.” 
Moreover, the Harshbarger Health Plan would “expand 
enforcement at both the state and local levels against youth 
access tobacco at retail outlets.” In addition, Defendant 
himself stated (in an April, 1999 op-ed piece in Zhe Boston 
Globe) that “the proportion of illegal sales to minors fell from 
48 percent to 8 percent in communities with a funded tobacco 
control program.” Defendant characterized the Massachusetts 
Department of Public Health’s tobacco control program as a 
“model for reducing smoking” that “has made a difference 
through education and enforcement.” 


28. Individually and cumulatively, the Challenged 
Restrictions impose excessive burdens on speech that will not 
materially advance the stated governmental interest of reducing 
teen smoking. The Challenged Regulations would undermine 
the manufacturers’ ability to communicate to adult consumers 
at the point of purchase, through the mails, and through 
nontobacco merchandising. These restrictions are in addition 
to the preexisting restrictions prohibiting or curtailing 
communication through, inter alia, broadcast media, outdoor 
billboards and transit signs, and stadium displays and 
sponsorships. Taken together, these provisions severely 
restrict the Plaintiffs’ ability to provide information to adult 
customers concerning Plaintiffs’ products. The Challenged 
Restrictions also excessively infringe on the rights of adult 
smokers to receive information about the manufacturers’ 
products. The benefits of the Challenged Regulations, if any, 


37 


can be otherwise achieved and do not justify the First 
Amendment restrictions imposed. 


29. Congress enacted the Federal Cigarette Labeling 
and Advertising Act (“F CLAA”) asa “comprehensive Federal 
program ... with respect to any relationship between smoking 
and health.” 15 US.C. § 1331. The “comprehensive” 
program was designed by Congress to ensure that “the public 
may be adequately informed about any adverse health effects 
of smoking,” id. § 133 1(1), while “commerce and the national 
economy” are “protected to the maximum extent consistent” 
with that objective, id § 1331(2). 


30. To ensure that the public is “adequately informed 
about any adverse health effects of cigarette smoking,” id. § 
/331 (1), FCLAA requires each package of cigarettes, and 
each cigarette advertisement, to Catry one of four rotating 
health warnings, see id. § 1333. 


31. FCLAA further explicitly provides that: “No 
requirement or prohibition based on smoking and health shall 
be imposed under State law with respect to the advertising or 
promotion of any cigarettes the packages of which are labeled 
in conformity with the provisions of § 1333 Id. § 1334(b). 


32. All of Plaintiffs tobacco products properly 
advertised and sold in the Commonwealth of Massachusetts 
conform to the requirements of FCLAA. 


33. The Challenged Regulations impose requirements 
or prohibitions based on smoking and health with respect to 
cigarettes which are packaged in conformity with FCLAA. 
The stated purpose of the Challenged Regulations to “address 
the incidence of cigarette smoking and smokeless tobacco use 
by children under legal age” has been repeatedly characterized 
by the defendant as a “public health” goal, as illustrated by the 
following examples: 


38 


: @ Ina statement made when he accepted the Agreement 
on behalf of the Commonwealth, the Attorney General 
identified the “reduc[tion of] tobacco use among 
children and adults” as a “key public health goal” in 
Massachusetts. (Prepared Remarks Accepting 
Tobacco Settlement, November 20, 1998) 


@ The Chief of the Attorney General’s Consumer 
Protection Bureau said that the proposed Challenged 
Regulations “would allow us to move aggressively to 
protec the health and safety of the children of 
Massachusetts in the event that Congress fails, on its 
own to create tough new marketing and advertising 
restrictions on the tobacco industry.” (Boston Globe, 
Feb. 14, 1998, at B 1). 


@ Ina public Internet “chat” session on April 22, 1997, 
the Attorney General stated that “there will be no 
settlement [of litigation between the Commonwealth 
and several tobacco companies] unless children can be 
fully protected, all public health issues are resolved 
fairly, including complete regulation of cigarette 
advertising. 


(http .: //wvww. townonline. com/~ewton/Speak/ 
C~hat/transcript42297. html) 


Claims for Relief 
COUNT I (FIRST AMENDMENT) 


34. Plaintiff incorporates and realleges each and every 
allegation contained in paragraphs 1 through 33 of this 
Amended Complaint as though fully set forth herein. 


35. Sections 21.04(2)(c), (2)(d), as well as 21.04(5) 
and (6) of the Challenged Regulations are null and void by 
reason of the First and Fourteenth Amendments to the United 
States Constitution. -They individually and cumulatively impose 
excessive burdens on constitutionally protected commercial 


39 


speech by the plaintiff to adult consumers. These burdens will 
not materially advance the stated governmental interest of 
reducing use of tobacco products by minors. The 
government’s asserted interest can be effectively served by 
alternative and more narrowly tailored means which do not 
restrict speech, and which do not Proscribe advertising — 
practices as “unfair or deceptive” without regard to the specific 
content of any particular advertisement. Moreover, these 
provisions are impermissibly overbroad. They restrict even 
non-commercial speech and they apply in settings that are 
physically and visually inaccessible to minors. 


36. Plaintiff has no adequate remedy at law. 
COUNT II (PREEMPTION) 


37. Plaintiff incorporates and realleges each and every 
allegation contained in paragraphs 1 through 36 of this 
Amended Complaint as though fully set forth herein. 


38. Sections 21.04(1)(a), (I)(c), (1)(d), (5) and (6) of 
the Challenged Regulations are null and void by reason of 
Article VI, Section 2 of the United States Constitution 
(“Supremacy Clause”) in that they are preempted by FCLAA. 


39. Plaintiff has no adequate remedy at law. 
COUNT III (COMMERCE CLAUSE AND DUE PROCESS) 
40. Plaintiff incorporates and realleges each and every 


allegation contained in paragraphs | through 39 of this 
Amended Complaint as though fully set forth herein. 


41. Sections 21.04(1)(a), (c) and (d) of the Challenged 
Regulations are null and void by reason of the Commerce 
Clause, Article 1, Section 8, Clause 3 of the United States 
Constitution, the Commerce Clause, and the Due Process 
Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment to the United States 
Constitution to the extent they purport to regulate or have the 
effect of regulating transactions and practices that do not occur 


40 


in Massachusetts, that do not involve Massachusetts consumers 
or that otherwise lack a sufficient nexus to Massachusetts, or 
to the extent they purport to regulate or have the effect of 
regulating national promotional programs from which 
Massachusetts, as a practical matter, cannot feasibly be 
excluded. 


42. Plaintiff has no adequate remedy at law. 


COUNT IV (EQUAL PROTECTION AND DUE PROCESS 
PROVISIONS OF THE FOURTEENTH AMENDMENT) 


43. Plaintiff incorporates and realleges each and every 
allegation contained in paragraphs I through 42 of this 
Amended Complaint as though fully set forth herein. 


44. Sections 21.04 (1)(a) and (d), and 21.04(2)(c) and 
(d) of the Challenged Regulations, as applied to retail outlets 
that are not accessible to individuals under the age of 18 years 
old, are not rationally related to the Commonwealth’s stated 
objective of restricting tobacco use by underage individuals, do 
not rationally further a legitimate state interest in preventing 
“unfair or deceptive acts or practices” and therefore violate the 
Equal Protection and Due Process provisions of the Fourteenth 
Amendment of the United States Constitution. 


45. Plaintiff has no adequate remedy at law. 
COUNT V (42 U.S.C. § 1983) 


46. Plaintiff incorporates and realleges each and every 
allegation contained in paragraphs 1 through 41 of this 
Amended Complaint as though fully set forth herein. 


a By promulgating and threatening to enforce the 
Challenged Regulations, Defendant has unlawfully and 
substantially deprived Plaintiff of rights secured by the United 
States Constitution. 


4) 


48. Defendant is a “person” under 42 U.S.C. § 1983 
who has acted under color of state law to deprive Plaintiff of 
rights secured by the United States Constitution. 


49. Plaintiff has no adequate remedy at law.

WHEREFORE, plaintiff prays that this Court: 


(A) enter a judgment declaring that 940 CMR. §§ 
21.04(1)(a), (1)(c) and (1)(d), 21.04(2)(c) and (2)(d)i 
21.04(5), and 21.04(6) are null and void;

(2)(d), 21.04(5) and i1.04(6);

pending final resolution of this action, enjoining Defendant and

C.M.R. §§ 21.04(1)(a), (1)(c) and (1)(d), 21.04(2)(c) and 
(2)(d), 21.04(5), and 21.04(6); 


(D) award plaintiff its reasonable attorneys; fees and 
costs} and 


(E) grant plaintiff such additional or different relief as

First Affirmative Def 


The Complaint, and each claim therein, fail to state a claim 
upon which relief can be granted. 


Second Affirmative Defense 
To the extent that any of the plaintiff's claims, expressly or 
impliedly, challenge the Attorney General’s conduct as 
violative of Massachusetts law or seek relief to require the 


Attorney General to conform his conduct to Massachusetts 
law, such claims are barred by the Eleventh Amendment.

this Court ,1) deny the plaintiff's request for a preliminary or 
permanent injunction; (2)-deny with prejudice all of the relief 
requested in the Complaint; (3) dismiss the Complaint with 
prejudice; and (4) grant such other relief as proper.

AMENDED COMPLAINT 
Nature of Action 
1. Plaintiffs, each a manufacturer or retailer of cigars sold 
throughout the United States including in Massachusetts, bring 


this action to declare null and void, and to enjoin enforcement 
of, 940 CMR § 22.00 et seq. (the “Challenged Regulations”) 


62 


(attached as Exhibit A) promulgated by Defendant, the 
Attorney General of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts 
The Challenged Regulations impose unconstitutional 
restrictions on Plaintiffs’ ability to package, advertise, promote, 
and sell cigars and little cigars, as defined in 940 CMR § 22.03 
(collectively, “cigars”’). 


The Challenged Regulations 
2. The two enumerated objectives of the Challenged 
Regulations are to inform “Massachusetts consumers” about 
the alleged health risks of cigar smoking and “to prevent access 
to [cigars] by underage consumers.” 940 CMR § 22.01. The 
Challenged Regulations purportedly seek to achieve those 


purposes by imposing the following restrictions on the 
packaging, advertising, promotion, and sale of cigars: 


(a) The Challenged Regulations restrict cigar 
packaging by: 


(i) requiring that machine-manufactured cigars, 
but not hand- rolled premium cigars, display a 
label that would occupy twenty-five percent of the 
front or top panel of the package, whichever is 
larger, stating one of two messages: (1) 
“Warning: Cigar Smoke Contains Carbon 
Monoxide And Nicotine, An Addictive Drug,” or 
(2) “Warning: Cigars Are Not A Safe Alternative 
To Cigarettes Or Smokeless Tobacco Products” 
(collectively, the “Massachusetts Attorney 
General’s Cigar Statement”), 940 CMR 
§ 22.04(1), (2); and 


(ii) requiring that manufacturers and distributors 
regularly rotate each label constituting the 
Massachusetts Attorney General’s Cigar 
Statement in alternating sequence on the packages 
of each brand of machine-manufactured cigars, 
940 CMR § 22.04(2) (d). 


63 


(b) The Challenged Regulations _ restrict Cigar 
advertising, promotion. and sale by: 


(i) requiring that retailers display one of the two 
labels constituting the Massachusetts Attorney 
General’s Cigar Statement wherever hand-rolled 
cigars are sold or machine-manufactured cigars 
are sold singly, 940 CMR § 22.06(2)(e): 


(i) requiring that twenty percent of the surface 
area of any cigar advertisement or statement 
promoting cigars be set aside for one of the two 
labels constituting the Massachusetts Attorney 
General’s Cigar Statement, 940 CMR § 22.05(1), 
(2); 


(iii) banning all Cigar advertising, regardless of 
whether it is deceptive or unfair, that is outdoors 
or visible from the outdoors within a thousand- 
foot radius of any elementary or secondary school, 
or any public playground, athletic field, swimming 
pool, baseball diamond, and/or basketball court 
(except for a single retailer announcement, 
“Tobacco Products Sold Here”), 940 CMR 
§ 22.06(5)(a); 


(iv) banning the placement of Cigar 
advertisements, cigar packages bearing a 
trademark, or any cigar promotional statement any 
Portion of which is placed lower than five feet 
from the floor, in retail establishments accessible 
to persons under eighteen years of age and located 
within a thousand-foot radius of any elementary or 
secondary school, or any public playground, 
athletic field, swimming pool, baseball diamond, 
and/or basketball court, 940 CMR § 22.06(5)(b); 


64 


(v) restricting the sale of cigars in any retail 
Outlet in any manner other than a face-to-face 
exchange, 940 CMR § 22 06(2)(a), 


(vi) banning the use of self-service displays in 
retail outlets, 940 CMR § 22 06(2\(c), 


(vil) requiring retail outlets to place all cigars out 
of the reach of all consumers, 940 CMR 
§ 22.06(2)(d), and 


(viii) banning cigar sales from vending machines in 
retail outlets, except from vending machines 
controlled by store personnel in facilities licensed 
to serve alcoholic beverages for consumption, 940 
CMR 8§ 22 06(2)(a), (4)(b) 


(c) The Challenged Regulations also ban the sampling 
of cigars, promotional giveaways of cigars, the sale of 
cigars with any gift or giveaway, the distribution of cigars 
by redemption of coupons, and the distribution of cigars 
through the mail, except where the person receiving the 
mail order cigars supplies a copy of a “valid government- 
issued identification.” 940 CMR §§ 22.06(1)(a), (c) & (d), 
22.06(4)(a). 


Nature of the Claims 
3. The Challenged Regulations, specifically including but 
not limited to §§ 22.04(1) and (2), 22.05(1) and (2), 
22.06(1 (a), 22.06(2)(c), (d), and (e), 22.06(5), and 22.06(6), 


violate the First and Fourteenth Amendments to the United 
States Constitution by: 


(a) restricting Plaintiffs’ rights to communicate with 
their customers and the public in the exercise of free 
commercial speech, and 


(b) requiring Plaintiffs and others to publish the 
Massachusetts Attorney General’s Cigar Statement. 


65 


**s * * 


7 The implementation of the Challenged Regulations will 
cause irreparable harm to Plaintiffs 


8 The Challenged Regulations are unconstitutional and 
invalid and should be preliminarily and permanently enjoined 

9 Plaintiff Consolidated Cigar Corporation 
(“Consolidated,” a “Plaintiff Manufacturer”) is a Delaware 
corporation with its principal place of business in Ft

outside Massachusetts, and its cigars are advertised. promoted, 
distributed and sold throughout the United States including in 
Massachusetts. Consolidated sells cigars under brand names 
including but not limited to Dutch Masters, Backwoods, 


Muriel, AyC, H. Upmann, Montecristo, Don Diego, and El 
Producto. 


10. Plaintiff General Cigar Co., Inc. (“General Cigar,” 
a “Plaintiff Manufacturer’’) is a Delaware corporation with its 
principal place of business in New York, New York. General 
Cigar manufactures its cigars outside Massachusetts, and its 
cigars are advertised, promoted, distributed and sold 
throughout the United States including in Massachusetts. 
General Cigar sells its cigars under brand names including but 
not limited to Macanudo, Cohiba, Partagas, Punch, Hoyo de 
Monterey, Bances, Top Stone, Villazon Deluxe, and Villa de 
Cuba.

including cigars, to adult consumers. Peretti operates one 
retail store near the Boston Common.

F ee All Clai 

20. Each Plaintiff Manufacturer’s brands of cigars have 
been brought from their place of manufacture outside 
Massachusetts to be sold in Massachusetts from before 1998 
continuously to the present. Each Plaintiff Manufacturer 
desires that its brands of cigars continue to be brought from 
other states and foreign countries into Massachusetts for sale. 


68 


21 In addition to selling cigars in its Massachusetts 
store, Plaintiff Retailer promotes the sale of cigars to 
individuals throughout the United States and around the world 
Plaintiff Retailer wishes to continue selling cigars outside 
Massachusetts 


22 The Challenged Regulations require compliance 
with labeling, advertising, and point-of-sale requirements for 
Plaintiff Manufacturers’ cigars to continue to be sold, and for 
Plaintiff Retailer to continue to sell cigars, in the 
Commonwealth of Massachusetts The Challenged 
Regulations become effective on August 1, 1999, although 
Defendant has stated that he will amend the Challenged 
Regulations to extend the effective date until February 1, 2000. 
There exists an actual controversy, justiciable in character, 
between Plaintiffs and Defendant as to whether the Challenged 
Regulations are lawful under the United States Constitution. 
Compliance with the challenged Regulations would violate 
Plaintiffs’ constitutional mghts and cause each Plaintiff 
irreparable injury, as would noncompliance. 


23. Plaintiffs engage in advertising and promotional 
activities that are intended to promote sales of cigars to adult 
_ smokers. Cigar manufacturers and retailers generally have 
employed several methods for advertising and promoting 
cigars, including but not limited to the use of valuable and 
distinctive trademarks and product packages and displays, print 
advertising, coupons, radio advertising, various forms of price 
offers, promotional materials in retail outlets, and distribution 
and display of promotional materials through other channels. 


24. Plaintiff Manufacturers’ cigars are advertised in 
national and regional publications, such as Chief Executive, 
Cigar Aficionado, Esquire, Field & Stream, Financial World, 
Forbes, Game & Fish, Golf Magazine, Gourmet, J&R 
Catalogue, Hemispheres (United Airlines in-flight magazine), 
Senior Golf, Smoke, Smokeshop, and Wine Spectator. 


69 


25 On-site advertising of cigars also constitutes an 
important means by which cigars are promoted. Convenience 
Stores, gas stations, drug stores, discount Stores, tobacconists, 
and other retail outlets promote sales of cigars, like most other 
products, by using self-service in-store displays and 
advertisements. In some cases, manufacturers and retail outlets 
engage in cooperative advertising where cigars are promoted 
along with other products on sale at the stores. To stimulate 
traffic into such stores and sales in the Store, it is important to 
communicate to adult consumers that they can purchase 
popular brands of cigars inside the store 


26. Retail product display is another critical means by 
which cigar manufacturers compete with one another and 
manufacturers and retailers communicate with adult 
consumers. In-store self-service displays of cigars provide 
price and other brand information that is crucial to the adult 
consumers’ choice among products. Because of major 
differences in the look and feel of cigar brands, adult 
consumers must have direct access to the cigar in its package 
to make comparisons with other cigars. For example, price 
comparison between brands is facilitated by comparing cigars 
and/or cigar packages side-by-side. Importantly, such displays 
permit the adult consumer to examine the product directly in 
the manner that the adult consumer chooses, and then make an 
immediate purchase decision. The manner in which adult 
consumers examine cigars at retail establishments and make 
their cigar purchase decisions has been essentially unchanged 
Over the years. 


* * *k * 


36. Although the Challenged Regulations refer to a 
“false perception that cigars are a safe alternative to 
cigarettes,” there are no findings that Plaintiffs’ cigar 
advertising and promotion in any way causes that claimed 
perception. There are no findings that the claimed perception 
iS a significant factor in cigar purchase decisions. There are no 


70 


findings that any cigar advertisements promote the use of 
cigars as a safer or healthier alternative to cigarette smoking. 
There are no findings that any cigar advertisements and 
promotions (such as cigar dinners) are not directed to adult 
consumers. 


37. The effect of the advertising restrictions in the 
Challenged Regulations is to ban all cigar advertisements that 
can be viewed from the outdoors in large sections of virtually 
every populated area of Massachusetts, regardless of whether 
these advertisements are deceptive or unfair. 


* * * * at 


41. The Challenged Regulations, specifically including 
but not limited to § 22.06(2)(c) and (d), require Plaintiff 
Retailer—even though it operates an adult-only store—and all 
other cigar retailers to place products out of reach of 
consumers and in a location accessible only to outlet personnel. 
As a result, Plaintiff Retailer’s customers will not be able to 
see, touch or smell the cigars it sells. To individuals who 
purchase cigars, such tactile interaction with the products is 
preferred. A cigar is not a fungible commodity, like a candy 
bar or a can of soda, that can be purchased without an 
individual inspection. Each brand of cigar has very individual 
characteristics. If Plaintiff Retailer’s and other retailers’ 
customers are denied the opportunity personally to compare 
and test individual cigars, the customary manner in which adult 
consumers have selected and purchased cigars over the years 
will be altered, and the product information available to adult 
consumers at the point of purchase will be severely limited. As 
a result, Plaintiff Retailer’s business, like those of other 
retailers, will be injured. 


42. Moreover, the Challenged Regulations, specifically 
including but not limited to § 22.06(5), require cigar retailers 
to remove all signs visible from the street advertising their 
stores and products (except for a non-descript statement 


71 


“Tobacco Products Sold Here”) if their stores operate fewer 
than 1000 feet from, among other things, a school, playground, 
or baseball diamond or basketball court. Such a restriction will 
have drastic effects on cigar retailers. For instance, because 
Plaintiff Retailer’s store is located less than 1000 feet from 
Boston Common, which contains baseball diamonds, Plaintiff 
Retailer will be forced to remove any promotional materials, 
advertisements, and signs from its front window. Asa result, 
Plaintiff Retailer, like many other cigar retailers, will not be 
able to communicate to its adult customers information about 
its cigars, including even the fact that it sells them. Therefore, 
Plaintiff Retailer’s business, like those of other retailers, will 
suffer. 


43. In addition, the Challenged Regulations, 
specifically including but not limited to §§ 22.06(2)(c) and (d), 
will compel Plaintiff Retailer and other retailers to redesign 
their stores. By effectively requiring such retailers to make all 
cigars inaccessible to their customers, the Challenged 
Regulations will oblige retailers to expand restricted areas 
(where physically possible to do SO), such as those behind the 
counter, or reduce the number and selection of cigars available 
for sale. 


44. Significantly, the Challenged Regulations, 
specifically including but not limited to § 22.06(3)(a), require 
retailers who, like Plaintiff Retailer, employ six or more 
individuals to use their own funds to hire a “secret shopper.” 
The secret shopper will periodically and surreptitiously visit the 
stores, including adult-only stores, to spy on employees to 
ensure compliance with the Challenged Regulations. Such an 
expense would dramatically diminish Plaintiff Retailer’s and 
other retailers’ profits. 


45. The Challenged Regulations, specifically including 
but not limited to § 22.06(2)(c) and (d), that require retailers 
to make cigars inaccessible to consumers, also severely affect 
Plaintiff Manufacturers. The practical effect of these 


72 


restrictions will be to make many brands and styles of cigars 
invisible to consumers in the store and effectively eliminate a 
critically important channel of communication with adult 
consumers. As a result, Plaintiff Manufacturers’ businesses 
will suffer. 


46. Defendant’s complete prohibition on the use of 
self-service displays, even where no issue of underage access 
is presented, also infringes on the rights of adult consumers to 
receive useful product information. 940 CMR § 22.06(2)(c). 
Such displays are a standard competitive tool available to 
marketers of other lawful consumer products. The prohibition, 
especially when combined with the other advertising 
restrictions contained in the Challenged Regulations, means 
that as a practical matter the adult consumer is deprived of 
information relevant to his choice at the very moment when 
that choice is being made and the information is of utmost 
importance. 


47. Plaintiffs, and cigar manufacturers and retailers 
generally, employ various marketing and promotional programs 
intended to attract adult cigar smokers and enhance the loyalty 
of adult smokers to particular brands. These programs play a 
significant role in the competition among cigar manufacturers. 
They also provide to adult consumers information about cigars 
that permits them to make informed choices about quality, 
price, and other competitive attributes of cigars. 


48. There are many steps Defendant and his agents 
could take to advance Defendant’s stated purpose of 
“prevent[ing] access to cigars by underage consumers” (940 
CMR § 22.01) without restricting or eliminating traditional 
channels of communication with adult consumers and 
customary methods of adult access to cigars at the time of the 
purchase decision, and without burdening interstate commerce. 
For example, it is illegal in Massachusetts for any person to sell 
tobacco products such as cigars to minors, or for any person 
(other than a parent or guardian) to give tobacco products to 


Ce 


73 


minors. Mass. Gen. Laws c.270, § 6. Vigorous enforcement 
of such laws can reduce youth smoking. The Commonwealth 
and its political subdivisions can also address underage 
smoking by means of educational programs. The 
Commonwealth has long claimed the effectiveness of its anti- 
tobacco media campaigns. For example, Defendant himself 
stated (in an April 6, 1999 op-ed piece in 7he Boston Globe) 
that as a result of local education and law enforcement efforts 
“the proportion of illegal sales to minors fell from 48 percent 
to 8 percent in communities with a funded tobacco control 
program.” Defendant characterized the Department of Public 
Health’s tobacco control program as a “model for reducing 
smoking” that “has made a difference through education and 
~enforcement.” Id. 


50. Implementation of the Challenged Regulations will 
cause irreparable harm to each Plaintiff who faces the denial of 
its free speech rights and the severe restriction of its ability to 
compete for retail sales. In addition, implementation of the 
Challenged Regulations will cause harm to adult cigar 
consumers who will be denied lawful access to, and 
information regarding, products they may wish to purchase. In 
addition, the profound interference with the interstate 
commerce in cigars caused by the Challenged Regulations will 
severely burden the business of Plaintiff Manufacturers. 


$1. The Attorney General of the Commonwealth of 
Massachusetts is a person within the meaning of 42 U.S.C. 
§ 1983 because Plaintiffs seek to enjoin him from taking 
actions in his official Capacity. 


Claims for Relief 
COUNT I (FREE SPEECH) 


aa. Plaintiffs incorporate and reallege each and every 
allegation contained in paragraphs 1 through 51 of this 
Complaint as though fully set forth herein. 


eth te t  a 


74 


53. The Challenged Regulations, specifically including 
but not limited to §§ 22.04(1) and (2), 22.05(1) and (2), 
22.06(1)(a), 22.06(2)(c), (d) and (e), 22.06(S), and 22.06(6), 
are null and void by reason of the First and Fourteenth 
Amendments to the United States Constitution because they 
impose prohibitions and restrictions on constitutionally 
protected commercial speech by Plaintiffs to adult consumers. 
The Defendant cannot show the prohibitions and restrictions 
will directly and materially advance and are reasonably related 
to the stated government interests. Defendant’s interest can be 
served effectively by means that do not uncor “(tutionally 
restrict speech. 


54. Plaintiffs have no adequate remedy at law.

First Affirmative Defense 


The Amended Complaint, and each claim therein, fail to 
state a claim upon which relief can be granted. 


Second Affirmative Defense 


To the extent that any of the plaintiffs’ claims, expressly or 
impliedly, in whole or in part, challenge the Attorney General’s 
conduct as violative of Massachusetts law or seek relief to 
require the Attorney General to conform his conduct to 


: " 


Massachusetts law, such claims are barred by the Eleventh 
Amendment. 


WHEREFORE, the defendant Thomas F. Reilly, as 
Attorney General of Massachusetts, respectfully requests that 
this Court (1) deny the plaintiffs’ request for a preliminary or 
permanent injunction, (2) deny with prejudice all of the relief 
requested in the Complaint; (3) dismiss the Complaint with 
prejudice, and (4) grant such other relief as is proper.

NNOLLY 
I, Gregory N. Connolly, depose and state the following: 


1. I am the Director of the Massachusetts Tobacco 
Control Program (MTCP) of the Massachusetts Department of 
Public Health. I have held this position since the formation of 
the Massachusetts Tobacco Control Program in 1993. From 
1986 to 1993 I was the director of the Office on Non-Smoking 
and Health. I have a D.M.D. from Tufts University School of 
Dental Medicine, and a Masters degree from the Harvard 
University School of Public Health. 


2. As Director of the MTCP, I control all aspects of the 
MTCP. 


0 
Ne ee ete 


million. 


4. The MTCP funds, Supports or implements a wide 
variety of programs in Massachusetts to: reduce tobacco 


and accurate copy of the CDC report Best Practices Jor 
Comprehensive Ti obacco Control Programs—A ugust 1999. 


and accurate copy of the portion of the CDC report State 
Tobacco Control Highlights—1999 that relates to 
Massachusetts. 


92 


targeted at youth. In addition to information on health risks, 
these advertisements presented messages to counter some of 
the beliefs that make tobacco use acceptable or desirable 
among young people. For example, one series of ads 
emphasized that young people can quickly become addicted to ~ 
nicotine, while another countered the idea that smoking makes 
one socially attractive. The Massachusetts Tobacco Control 
Program media campaign budget was $16 million for fiscal 
year 1994, $14 million for fiscal year 1995, $13 million for 
fiscal year 1996, $11.70 million for fiscal year 1997, and 
$11.21 million for fiscal year 1998. The Massachusetts 
Tobacco Control Program media campaign budget is $11 
million for fiscal year 1999. 


6. The MTCP supports and funds a variety of community- 
based programs, particularly with local Boards of Health and 
local Health Departments. Some of the MTCP funding 
provides technical assistance and support for enactment and 
enforcement of local ordinances and regulations designed to 
make it harder for youth to buy tobacco products from retail 
establishments and vending machines, and to protect the public 
from environmental tobacco smoke. Boards of Health and 
Health Department Programs also provide important services 
to the general public such as smoking cessation services. 


7. The population affected by local restrictions on youth 
access to tobacco has nsen dramatically since the MTCP effort 
began. For example, 177 Massachusetts cities and towns, 
accounting for more than three-quarters of the population of 
the Commonwealth, now require merchants who sell tobacco 
products to be licensed. This represents a dramatic change 
over the lifetime of the MTCP, as less than 5 percent of the 
population was covered by such requirements in 1992. Local 
laws limiting free-standing “self-service” displays of tobacco 
products have been adopted almost as broadly in communities, 
representing more than two-thirds of Massachusetts residents. 
Also, as of June 1998, 87 towns have completely banned 


93 


vending machines, 75 require the use of “lockout” devices on 
vending machines, and 56 limit vending machines to locations 
that are not accessible to youth. 


8. During the time period from January of 1994 to June of 
1998, the efforts of the local Boards of Health and Health 
Departments, together with other local-MTCP-funded 
programs, to monitor and enforce youth access provisions 
included: 


a. 46,568 checks of retail premises to make sure that they 
had appropriate signs indicating the prohibition on sales to 
minors; 


b. 31,603 controlled buying attempts, in which youth 
under age 18 attempted to purchase cigarettes and reported 
on the results; 


c. 7,561 checks of compliance with vending machine 
restrictions. 


9. At the time of the start of the MTCP warnings were 
given out to merchants for most violations, and the citation rate 
in 1994 (which is the percentage of observed violations which 
result in a citation) was just 13%. By the first half of 1998, the 
citation rate had climbed to 53%. 


10. From January of 1994 to June of 1998 illegal sales 
have resulted in a total of 1,910 citations and $139,325 in 
levied fines. In March-May of 1994, 48% of all attempts 
resulted in illegal sales of cigarettes. Since that time, retailer 
compliance has improved, and the violation rate was a 
Statewide average of 19.3% in 1998 for both MTCP-funded 
and non-MTCP-funded communities, and under 10% in 
MTCP-funded communities in April-June of 1998. 


11. In addition to the compliance checks performed by 
the local Boards of Health and Health Departments, the 
Massachusetts Tobacco Control Program carries out 
cc..npliance checks as part of a project funded by the U.S. Food 


94 


and Drug Administration. These compliance checks were 
performed in communities across Massachusetts, even where 
there was no local tobacco control program. Over 5,000 
compliance checks were carried out in 1998, and over 8,000 
compliance checks have already been performed in 1999. The 
average non-compliance rate was 22.8% in 1998, and the 
average non-compliance rate for the first six months of 1999 is 
29.7%. 


12. The MTCP works together with the Massachusetts 
Department of Education to support comprehensive health 
education in public schools, including education on tobacco 
prevention and smoking cessation. The MTCP also creates 
and funds other educational initiatives. For example, a new 
MTCP initiative called “Smoke Screeners” teaches students to 
become more critical movie viewers by becoming more aware 
of how tobacco is used in Hollywood films and better able to 
see through the “smokescreen” glamorizing smoking. The 
Smoke Screeners program reached out to the high-risk 11-14 
year olds in more than 1,000 middle schools and youth centers 
throughout Massachusetts. 


13. Outside of the classroom, numerous MTCP 
activities inform youth of tobacco risks and involve them in 
positive peer efforts. Creative activities carried out by local 
programs are designed both to provide information and to 
engage youth in positive anti-smoking leadership activities. 
For example, members of MTCP-funded youth programs have 
sponsored or participated in over 3,200 community tobacco 
education and prevention events, such as public speaking 
engagements and recreational or cultural events. In Fiscal Year 
1998 alone almost 900 events drew a total attendance of over 
160,000 people. 


14. The MTCP funds statewide support service 
programs that disseminate tobacco educational materials; raise 
public awareness of the health hazards associated with tobacco 
use and of tobacco industry activities that promote use; 


95 


educate local cities and towns about tobacco control legal and 
policy issues that may affect their residents; and provide easily 
accessible assistance to smokers and their families. These 
programs include a clearinghouse of educational materials, a 
smokers’ quit counseling line, and training programs. 


15. According to the Massachusetts Adult Tobacco 
Surveys, which were conducted under the direction and 
supervision of the MTCP, 63% of current adult smokers in 
Massachusetts started smoking before they were 18 years old, 
and only 12% began after age 2]. 


16. In 1998, the MTCP conducted a survey of the 
advertisements visible on more than 3,000 storefronts in 
Massachusetts in 125 cities and towns across the 
Commonwealth. The results of this Statewide advertising 
survey were published in a report entitled “Operation 
Storefront.” Operation Storefront found: 


a. Tobacco advertising made up 52% of the more than 
20,000 advertisements visible from outside retail 
establishments surveyed from February through April 1998: 


b. The four brands which surveys by the CDC have shown 
are the most popular brands among youth (Marlboro, 
Newport, Camel, and Winston) (accounting for 93% of 
youth smoking), were the most heavily advertised brands 
visible on storefronts. These four brands accounted for 
more than two-thirds (68%) of all tobacco ads; 


c. Stores within 1,000 feet of schools displayed more 
tobacco advertisements per vendor than stores farther 
away. The average number of tobacco advertisement was 
5.49 for stores near schools, and 3.96 for other stores; 


d. Stores in poorer communities and minority 
communities carry more cigarette advertising per store than 
those in more affluent and non-minority communities. 


96 


17. In or about 1991, I contacted The Tobacco 
Institute to request a copy of their voluntary advertising and 
promotion code. The document attached as Exhibit C to this 
affidavit is a true and accurate copy of the document I received 
from the Tobacco Institute, entitled “Cigarette Advertising and 
Promotion Code.”

Best Practices for Comprehensive

CDC 


U.S. Department of Health and Human Services

* * *k * 


Efficacy of Comprehensive Tobacco Control Programs: 
California and Massachusetts 


related diseases, school programs, cessation programs, 
enforcement, and counter-marketing program elements are 
based primarily upon published evidence-based guidelines. 
Other program categonies rely mainly upon the evidence of the 
efficacy of the large-scale and sustained efforts of two States 
(California and Massachusetts) that have been funding 
comprehensive tobacco prevention and control programs using 
State tobacco excise taxes Increasing excise taxes on 
cigarettes reduces tobacco consumption rates. But more 
importantly, when the excise taxes support effective 
community, media, and school programs to prevent tobacco 
use, decreases in per capita consumption will continue even if 
industry lowers tobacco Prices to preexcise tax values.

Exhibit “C” 
Cigarette Advertising and Promotion Code 


The Tobacco Institute 


98 


Cigarette smoking is an adult custom. Children should not 
smoke. Laws prohibiting the sale of cigarettes to minors 
should be strictly enforced. The cigarette manufacturers 
advertise and promote their products only to adult smokers. 
They support the enactment and enforcement of state laws 
prohibiting the sale of cigarettes to persons under 18 years of 
age. 


The cigarette manufacturers have adopted the following 
Code to emphasize their policy that smoking is solely for 
adults. 


* * * * 


Advertising 
1. Cigarette advertising shall not appear— 


(a) in publications directed primarily to those under 21 
years of age, including school, college or university media 
(such as athletic, theatrical or other programs), comic 
books or comic supplements; or 


(b) on billboards located within 500 feet of any 
elementary school, junior high school or high school or any 
children’s playground. 


2. No payment shall be made by any cigarette manufacturer or 
any agent thereof for the placement of any cigarette, 
cigarette package, or cigarette advertisement as a prop in 
any movie produced for viewing by the general public. 


3. No one depicted in cigarette advertising shall be or appear 
to be under 25 years of age. 


4 Cigarette advertising shall not suggest that smoking is 
essential to social prominence, distinction, success or 
sexual attraction, nor shall it picture a person smoking in an 
exaggerated manner. 


eI I a Te eR Bi p9 WIE PLE ETS Oe OER Pe Be a haa iti kn wh Da ae 


ee Pee . 
Vis 2 
Se ne Ley 


99 


Cigarette advertising may picture attractive, healthy 
looking persons provided there is no suggestion that their 
attractiveness and good health is due to cigarette smoking. 


Cigarette advertising shall not depict as a smoker anyone 
who is or has been well known as an athlete, nor shall it 
show any smoker Participating in, or obviously just having 
Participated in, a physical activity requiring stamina or 
athletic conditioning beyond that of normal recreation. 


No sports or celebrity testimonials shall be used or those of 
others who would have special appeal to Persons under 2] 
years of age. 


ee & & 


Sampling 


l. 


Persons who engage in sampling shall refuse to give a 
sample to any person whom they know to be under 21 
years of age or who, without reasonable identification to 
the contrary, appears to be less than 2] years of age. 


Sampling shall not be conducted in or on public streets, 
sidewalks or parks, except in places that are open only to 
persons to whom cigarettes lawfully may be sold. 


Cigarette product samples shall not Otherwise be 
distributed in any public place within two blocks of any 
centers of youth activities. such as playgrounds, schools, 
college campuses, or fraternity or sorority houses. 


* kk * 


Definitions 


I. 


“Advertising” means all forms of advertising including 
vehicle decals, posters, pamphlets, matchbook covers and 
point of purchase materials in the United States, Puerto 
Rico, and U.S. territorial POSsessions. 


a 


100 


2. “Sampling” means giving or distributing without charge 
packages of cigarettes in a public place for commercial 
advertising purposes (“cigarette samples”), but does not 
include isolated offerings of complimentary packages or the 
distribution of such packages ro wholesale or retail 
customers or to company shareholders or employees in the 
normal course of business.

ATTORNEY GENERAL’S STATEMENT OF 
MATERIAL FACTS AS TO WHICH THERE 
IS NO GENUINE ISSUE TO BE TRIED 
PURSUANT TO LOCAL RULE 56.1 


* * * * 


15. In January 1999, the Attorney General 
promulgated regulations pursuant to M.G.L. c. 93A, §2 (c) to 
proscribe certain unfair and deceptive acts and practices that 
promote the illegal sale of tobacco products to persons under 
18 years of age. 940 Code Mass. Reg. § 21.00, ef seq. and 940 
Code Mass. Reg. § 22.00, ef seg. (Copies attached). The 
Attorney General’s regulations were originally proposed, and 
published in the Massachusetts Register, on May 1, 1998. 
Public hearings were held in Boston on May 20, 1998 and in 
Springfield on May 21, 1998, eliciting testimony supporting 
and opposing the regulations from 41 individuals and 
organizations. In addition, approximately 100 written 
comments on the proposed regulations were submitted to the 
Attorney General by various individuals and organizations. 
Affidavit of Michael G. Hering (“Hering Aff.”’) 4 94. 


Support for the Attorney General’s Regulations 


16. ~ In 1994, the United States Surgeon General 


published a report entitled Preventing Tobacco Use Among 
Young People: A Report of the Surgeon General. U.S. 

Department of Health and Human Services, Public Health 
Service, Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, National 
Center for Chronic Disease Prevention and Health Promotion, 
Office on Smoking and Health, 1994. (“1994 Surgeon General 
Report”). That report included a chapter, at pages 157-203, 
which reviewed evidence relating cigarette advertising to youth 
smoking initiation and stated: “Cigarette advertising appears to 
affect young people’s perceptions of the pervasiveness, image, 
and function of smoking. Since misperceptions in these areas 
constitute psychosocial risk factors for the initiation of 


103 


smoking, cigarette advertising appears to increase young 
people’s risk of smoking.” Hering Aff., Ex. 1, p. 195. 


17. The 1994 Surgeon General’s Report found that 
among all persons who had ever been daily smokers, 81 9% 
first tried a cigarette before age 18. Hering Aff, Ex. 1, p. 65. 


18. In 1995, the Food and Drug Administration 


behavior, and 2) a Positive effect of stringent advertising 
measures On smoking rates and on youth smoking... . Finally, 
examples of specific campaigns directed at young people 


consumption. Therefore, the agency finds that stringent 
restrictions on advertising are essential if smoking by 
adolescents is to be reduced.” Hering Aff, Ex. 2, p. 41334. 


104 


advertising plays a material role in the decision by those under 
18 to use tobacco products.” Hering Aff., Ex. 3, p. 44466. 


20 In 1994, the National Academy of Science’s™ 
Institute of Medicine issued a report entitled Growing Up 
Tol foe 3 ag Nicotine Addiction in Child , 
Youths. that reviewed the evidence relating cigarette 
advertising to youth smoking initiation. p. 105-139. The report 
stated “Tobacco advertising is characterized by images and 
themes that are especially appealing to adolescents, and some 
are appealing to children. In addition, a large proportion of 
promotional expenditures associate use of tobacco with 
activities and products that are attractive to children and 
youths. The sheer amount of expenditures for advertising and 
promotion assures that young people will be exposed to these 
messages on a massive scale.” The report also states, “The 
images typically associated with advertising and promotion 
convey the message that tobacco use is a desirable, socially 
approved, safe and healthful, and widely practiced behavior 
among young adults, whom children and youths want to 
emulate. As a result, tobacco advertising and promotion 
undoubtedly contribute to the multiple convergent pyschosocial 
influences that lead children and youths to begin using these 
products and to become addicted to them.” Hering Aff., Ex. 4, 
p. 130-31. 


21. A review study entitled “How Many Adolescents 
Start Smoking Each Day in the United States?” by Gilpin, 
Elizabeth A., et al., was published in the Journal of Adolescent 
Health 1999:25:248-255. The study found that nationwide 
4,824 adolescents first experiment with cigarettes every day. 
Adolescent is defined as between 11 and 17 years old. The 
study also found that 1,975 under-age U.S. adolescents

Outdoor Tobacco Advertising in Massachusetts 


22. A study entitled “Outdoor Tobacco Advertising in 
Six Boston Neighborhoods. Evaluating Youth Exposure” by 
Pucci, et. al., was published in the American Journal of 
Preventive Medicine, 1998, 15(2). The study examined the 
prevalence, type and Proximity to public schools of all 
Stationary outdoor tobacco advertising in six Boston 
neighborhoods. The Study found that “youth in the six 
neighborhoods are heavily exposed to stationary, outdoor 
cigarette advertising. This exposure is intense in areas close to 
public schools, and more intense in neighborhoods with more 
children, with significant Black and Hispanic/Latino 
populations, and with low Socioeconomic status. __ 32% of 
the advertising units [at retail establishments] were less than 
3.5 feet from the ground or at children’s eye level.” Hering 
Aff., Ex. 57.

1998. The study surveyed more than 3000 retail outlets in 125 
cities and towns in Massachusetts. The study found: 


a. “Tobacco advertisements made up 52% of the more 
than 20,000 advertisements visible to youth from outside retail 
establishments surveyed...” 


b. “Brands smoked by youth (Marlboro, Camel, Newport, 
Winston) were also the most heavily advertised brands, 
accounting for more than two-thirds of all tobacco ads.” 


c. “Storefronts located within 1000 feet of a school were 
more likely to display more tobacco advertisements per retail 
vendor, and displayed more ads per vendor, than storefronts 
located farther away from schools. F ifty-two percent of retail 


106 


ads near schools were for tobacco, while only 48% percent 
away from schools were tobacco ads.” 


d. “The average number of tobacco advertisements was

displays during their daily activities.” Hering Aff, Ex. 58; 
Affidavit of Greg Connolly (“Connolly Aff.”) at. § 16.
