25. Historical studies have analyzed variations in the 
kind and amount of cigarette advertising in the past and 
compared these advertising variations with changes in smoking 
initiation among youths. Some of these studies examined 
specific cigarette marketing campaigns and concluded that they 
were associated with increased smoking behavior among young 
people including minors. Hering Aff., Ex. 5 - 9. 


26. A study entitled “Trends in Adolescent Smoking 
Initiation in the United States: Is Tobacco Marketing an 
Influence?” by Gilpin, E., and Pierce, J., was published in 
Tobacco Control. 6(2):122-127 (1997). The study analyzes 
survey and other data, and states: “Results--From 1979 to 
1984, adolescent [smoking] initiation rates decreased, but 
increased thereafter... Cigarette price increased throughout the 
decade as did tobacco marketing expenditures, especially for 
coupons, value-added items, and promotional allowances. 
Conclusion— Availability of cheaper cigarettes is not likely to 
be a cause of increased smoking initiation by adolescents. 
Although other influences cannot be ruled out, we suspect that 
the expanded tobacco marketing budget, with its increased 
emphasis on tactics that may be particularly pertinent to young 


107 


people, affected adolescent initiation rates.” Hering Aff., Ex. 
5, p.122. 


Z. A study entitled “A Historical Analysis of Tobacco 
Marketing and the Uptake of Smoking by Youth in the United 
States: 1890-1977” by Pierce, J., and Gilpin, E., was published 
in Health Psychology. 14(6): 500-508 (1995). The study 
reports the results of a series of Surveys and other data, Stating: 
“Our initiation rate trend (slope) analysis clearly show that 
marked increases in the rate of smoking uptake in the particular 
gender group targeted by these [advertising] campaigns were 
coincident with the beginning of each. Such an offect was not 
observed among the nontargeted gender” p.5. [Following a 
historical analysis culminating in the period ending in 1977] 
“Thus, it would appear that the effectiveness of tobacco 
marketing practices in encouraging nonsmokers to start 
smoking mav now be limited to the adolescent population.” 
Hering Aff, Ex. 6, p.8.. 


28. A study entitled “Trends in smoking initiation 
among adolescents and young adults -- United States, 1980- 
1989” by Cummings KM, Shah D, Shopland DR was published 
in the Centers for Disease Control, Morbidity and Mortality 
Weekly Report, 1995: 44:521-5 The study reports the results 
of survey data stating: “Among adolescents [age 14-17], the 
smoking initiation rate decreased slightly from 1980 (5.4%) 
through 1984 (4.7%) and then increased through 1989 
(5.5%)... In comparison, among young adults, initiation rates 
declined throughout the 1 980s.” “The increase in rates of 
smoking initiation among adolescents during 1985-1989 may 
reflect increased real expenditures for cigarette advertising and 
Promotion. The increase in rates occurred during a period 
when real expenditures for total cigarette advertising and 
Promotion deubled, and expenditures for cigarette promotion 
more than quadrupled.” Hering Aff, Ex. 7. 


29. A study entitled “Smoking Initiation by Adolescent

108 


Advertising” by Pierce, J., Lee, L., and Gilpin, E., was 
published in the Journal of the American Medical Association. 
271(8): 608-611 (1994). The study reports the results of

data, stating: “Results.--In 18 to 20-year-old women, 
[smoking] initiation rates peaked in the early 1960s and steadily 
declined thereafter. In girls younger than 18 years, smoking 
initiation increased abruptly around 1967, when tobacco

introduced. ..Conclusions.--The tobacco advertising campaigns 
targeting women, which were launched in 1967, were 
associated with a major increase in smoking uptake that was 
specific to females younger than the legal age for purchasing 
cigarettes.” Hering Aff., Ex. 8, p. 608.

39. A study entitled “Reinforcing Effects of Cigarette 
Advertising on Under-age Smoking” by Aitken, P.P. and D.R. 
Eadie was published in the British Journal of Addiction. 85: 
399-412 (1990). The study reported the results of interviews 
with 848 Glasgow children aged between 11 and 14 years. The 
study stated: “There were consistent differences between 
smokers and non-smokers. Smokers tended to be more adept 
at recalling, recognizing and _ identifying cigarette 
advertisements. This suggests that they pay more attention to 
cigarette advertising. Smokers also tended to be more 
appreciative of cigarette advertising. Moreover, this greater 
awareness and appreciation of cigarette advertising was 
independent of other important predictors of under-age 
smoking, such as smoking by peers, siblings and parents. 
These findings, taken in conjunction with previous research, 
indicate that cigarette advertising is reinforcing under-age 
smoking.” Hering Aff., Ex. 16, p. 399.

53. A study entitled “Influence of Tobacco Marketing 
and Exposure to Smokers on Adolescent Susceptibility to 
Smoking” Evans, Nicola, et al. was published in the Journal of 
the National Cancer Institute. 87(20): 1538-1545 (1995). The 
study reported the results of a survey of 3,536 adolescents [12- 
17 years old] who had never smoked. The study found: 
“Receptivity to tobacco advertising and exposure to smokers 
were independently associated with susceptibility to smoking, 
but the relationship appeared stronger for recentivity to 
advertising. Adolescents exposed to family members and 
peers...who smoked were 1.89 (95% confidence interval...) 
times as likely to be susceptible [to smoking], whereas 
adolescents who scored 4 or more on the Index of Receptivity 
to Tobacco Marketing [recognition of cigarette advertising 
messages, having a favorite cigarette advertisement, owning or 
willingness to use a promotional item, and other factors] were 
3.91... times as likely to be susceptible [to smoking] as those 
who scored 0. Even adolescents who scored 2...were 2.03 
times as likely to be susceptible...Conclusion: Our results 
support the hypothesis that tobacco marketing may be a 
stronger current influence in encouraging adolescents to initiate 


117 


the smoking uptake process than exposure to peer or family 
smokers or sociodemographic variables including perceived 
school performance.” Hering Aff, Ex. 29, p. 1538. 


54. A study entitled “Smoking Behavior of

65. Several longitudinal studies have concluded that 
nonsmoking youths who either approved of cigarette 
advertising or reported that cigarette advertisements made 
smoking appear attractive to them were significantly more 
likely to start smoking than youths who had more negative 
responses to cigarette advertising at baseline. Other 
longitudinal studies have found that youths who are responsive 
to cigarette advertising of participate in tobacco promotions 
are more likely to experiment with smoking or to progress to 
established smoking. Hering Aff., Ex. 39, 41 - 44.

Hering Aff. Ex. 39.

67. A study entitled “Tobacco Industry Promotion of 
Cigarettes and Adolescent Smoking” by Pierce, John P., et al., 
was published in the Journal of the American Medical 
Association. 279(7): 511-515 (1998). The study reported the 
results of an initial survey of adolescents in 1993 and follow up 
survey in 1996. The study reinterviewed 1,752 adolescents in 
1996 who had been categorized as “never smokers who were 
not susceptible to smoking” when first interviewed in 1993 to 
evaluate the association between receptivity to tobacco 
advertising and promotional activities and progress in the 
smoking uptake process. The study siates: “While having a 
favorite advertisement in 1993 predicted which adolescents 
would progress [in the smoking uptake process] by 1996, 
Possession or willingness to use a promotional item was even 
more strongly associated with future progression. From these 
data, we estimate that 34% of all experimentation in California 
between 1993 and 1996 can be attributed to tobacco 
promotional activities. Nationally, this would be over 700,000 
adolescents each year.” Hering Aff, Ex. 41. 


68. A study entitled “Predisposing Effects of Cigarette 

; Advertising on Children’s Intention to Smoke When Older” by 
Aitken, P.P., edt al., was published in the British Journal of 
Addiction. 86:383-390 (1991). The study reported the results 

of 'a survey of 640 Glasgow children, initially aged between 11 

and 14 years, who were interviewed twice, with approximately 

one year between interviews. The Study stated: “Children 
whose intentions to smoke when older became more positive 
between the two interviews tended to be more aware of 
Cigarette advertising at the time of the first interview 


122 


(compared with children whose intentions to smoke were 
negative at both interviews). Children whose intentions to 
smoke became more negative between the interviews tended to 
be less appreciative of cigarette advertisements at the time of 
the first interview (compared with children whose intentions to 
smoke were positive at both interviews). Since both groups 
differed from their respective contrast groups before their 
declared intentions changed, these findings support the view 
that cigarette advertising has predisposing as well as 
reinforcing effects on children’s attitudes and behaviour with 
respect to smoking.” Hering Aff., Ex. 42, p. 383.

1990;152: 117-24. The study reported the results of an initial 
survey of 2366 Australian students in their 7th year of school 
(average age 12) with two years of follow-up surveys. The 
study states: “[t]he children’s perceived responses to cigarette 
advertising showed the strongest and most consistent evidence 
of an effect on the uptake of smoking by children who initially 
were non-smokers.” -Hering Aff, Ex. 43.

71. Studies of smoking rates in countries with and 


without significant cigarette advertising restrictions have 
generally found small, but significant reductions in both overall 
smoking rates, and in youth smoking rates, following these 
advertising restrictions. Hering Aff., Ex. 3, 45 - 46, 89. 


72. A review study entitled “Tobacco Advertising: 
Economic Theory and International evidence” by Saffer, Henry 
et al., was published in NBER Working Paper Series. The 
paper analyzed 31 studies that looked at the effects of 


advertising on cigarette consumption, conducted between 1925 
and 1990. The authors concluded that “[t}he primary 
conclusion of this research is that tobacco advertising increases 
tobacco consumption.” Hering Aff, Ex. 89. 


** * * 


74. A report entitled “Health or Tobacco--An End to 
Tobacco Advertising and Promotion,” by the Toxic Substances 
Board, Wellington, New Zealand was issued in May 1989. The 
report reviewed the effect of advertising restrictions in 33 
countries and concluded that there was a correlation between 
the degree of restrictions imposed in each country and decline 
in tobacco use. The report stated: “When countries were 
grouped according to the degree of governmental restriction of 
tsbacco promotion, the greater the degree of restriction, the 
greater the average annual fall in tobacco consumption. This 
was also true for the-rate of decrease in the percentage of 
adults and young people who smoke.” Hering Aff., Ex. 46, p. 
64. 


75. The FDA analyzed the academic research on the 
effect of restrictions on tobacco advertising in other countries. 
Proposed Rule, Fed. Reg. 41333-34:; FDA Final Rule, Fed. 
Reg. 44490-44493. The FDA then stated: “Based on the 
foregoing, FDA-finds that the international experience provides 
empirical evidence that restrictions on tobacco advertising, 
when given appropriate Scope and when fully implemented, will 
reduce cigarette and smokeless tobacco use among children 
and adolescents under the age of 18.” FDA Final Rule, Fed. 
Reg. 44493. Hering Aff, Ex. 3.

the three most popular with adolescents. Hering Aff, Ex. 9.

Dr. Edward V. Morse is a behavioral scientist, trained at 
Cornell University. He received his Ph.D. in 1971. A major 
focus of his research has been health-related social patterns, 
and research methodology. After graduating from Cornell, Dr. 
Morse accepted an academic appointment at Tulane University 
as an Assistant Professor in 1971. He became an Associate 
Professor with tenure in 1977 and is now a full member of the 
Graduate Faculty at Tulane. Dr. Morse was awarded a 
Fulbright Scholarship in 1981 to conduct research and teach in 
the area of behavioral medicine in Bogota, Colombia. Dr. 
Morse served as University Chairman of the department of 
Sociology from 1977 to 1985. Since being at Tulane, he has 


137 


held a range of other positions, including Adjunct Associate 
Professor Department of Biostatistics and Epidemiology, 
Director of the Center for Applied Social Research, Clinical 
Professor of Psychiatry, LSUMC, New Orleans, Louisiana, and 
Adjunct Associate Professor of Pediatrics, Tulane University 
Medical School, New Orleans, Louisiana. For the last twenty- 


nine years, Dr. Morse has played an active research role in the 
scientific community. 


Since 1990, Dr. Morse has received funding for work on 
more than 25 grants and contracts in the area of behavioral 
medicine. The vast majority of his research funding has come 
from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, the 
National Institute of Allergic and Infectious Diseases, the 
National Institute of Mental Health, the American Association 
of Retired People, and W.T. Grant Foundation. During the last 
decade, he has devoted the greater portion of his research 
effort to the study of factors underlying high-risk behavior 
patterns of adolescents and younger adults. Dr. Morse is 
currently the Principal or Co-Principal Investigator of over 
$1,000,000 in grants’ funds. Dr Morse’s research findings 
have resulted in publication in a wide range of professional 
peer-reviewed journals, nationally and internationally presented 
posters, and nationally and internationally presented papers. 
He teaches a graduate level course in behavioral medicine and 
annually lectures on specific behavioral medicine topics to the 


Tulane School of Public Health and Tropical Medicine and the 
Tulane School of Medicine. 


Dr. Morse has served as a consultant to the White House, 
the United Sates Department of Health and Human Services, 
the State of Louisiana (public schools and school-based health 
care) and other private and public agencies to assist in the 
translation of research findings into public policy which 
produce more effective programmatic interventions. Dr. 
Morse’s curriculum vitae, which presents his professional, and 
academic activities in greater detail is attached. 


138 


Dr. Morse’s opinions and testimony are based on his 
academic training, primary and secondary research experiences, 
field research experience, publications, consulting, the scientific 
literature on the issues, and ongoing review of other pertinent 
materials and other information reasonably relied upon by 
members of his profession. He also bases his opinion upon his 
review of advertisements, public opinion polls and surveys, 
government documents, and publications by the National 
Institutes of Health and Centers for Disease Control and 
Prevention. A list of principal references relied upon by Dr. 
Morse is attached. Dr. Morse’s work in this case is ongoing. 
He may review additional documents and discovery produced 
or to be produced by the defendant and may supplement this 
report. Dr. Morse will rely on the work and opinions of other 
experts in this case. Dr. Morse may also offer testimony in 
response to related issues raised by the Commonwealth. 


Dr. Morse will testify about high-risk behavior patterns 
among children and adolescents. Patterns of high-risk health 
behavior tend to occur in cycles and reflect fads or trends that 
differ within subgroups (e.g. males vs. females, 12 year-olds vs. 
16 year-olds, etc.). As a group, a significant portion of 
adolescents is likely to engage in unsafe premarital sex, drive 
over the posted speed limit, not use seat belts, experiment with 
non-prescription street drugs and alcohol, smoke cigarettes, 
operate equipment without appropnate head safety wear, carry 
firearms, fight in school, and disregard proper nutritional intake 
and appropriate daily exercise. High-risk health behavior 
patterns may be placed on a continuum from behaviors with 
high probabilities of resulting in immediate health 
compromising consequences, to those with low probabilities of 
even a small negative long-term impact on health. As 
adolescents grow up, the majority greatly reduce or eliminate 
many high-risk health behavior patterns, including tobacco use. 


— NN __ ta 


139 


Dr. Morse will also offer his opinion, based on decades of 
research and observation, that children’s and adolescents’ 
behavior patterns related to smoking are primarily and directly 
influenced by their peers’ and parents’ smoking behavior 
patterns. Past “focus group” data collected by the FDA, as 
well as national and international studies, demonstrate that 
smoking behavior patterns, similar to other high-risk health 
behavior patterns, are influenced by peers, parents, siblings, 
extended family members, and significant others in their local 
communities. Further, these same sources of influence play a 


predominant role in shaping the adolescents’ choice to continue 
to smoke or abstain from smoking. 


There is no credible social science research that 
demonstrates that adolescents’ exposure to commercial 
marketing, advertising, promotions or sponsorships of any 
cigarette company has any impact on the decision to start 
smoking. Further, research findings suggest that adolescents’ 
exposure to commercial marketing, advertising, or promotions 
and sponsorships Statistically do not account for any pattern of 
smoking. If cigarette advertising had the impact on the 
smoking behavior patterns of children and young adults that it 
is alleged to have, one would expect smoking patterns of 
young people to be homogeneous (e. g. all adolescents exposed 
to cigarette advertising would smoke). Data from the 25-year 
trend study of substance use patterns conducted by the 
University of Michigan, fails to Support such an assertion. 
Instead, the data clearly show that. by age, grade in school, 
region of the country, gender and ethnicity, patterns of 


smoking vary in ways that have no relationship to cigarette 
advertising and promotion. 


In fact, these patterns, which cannot be explained by 
€xposure to advertising, are much more closely associated with 
social and psychological factors such as peer and family 
€xposure. Indeed, these same social and psychological factors 
predict other high-risk behaviors, such as use of illicit drugs, 


140 


that are not advertised in any fashion. Thus, efforts to alter 
adolescents’ smoking behavior by limiting their exposure to 
cigarette advertising are not likely to have any significant or 
meaningful impact on the smoking initiation patterns of 
adolescents. 


Based on the literature and past experience, it has been 
demonstrated that the best way to reduce the incidence and 
prevalence of adolescent smoking is to enforce the state laws 
which prohibit minors from purchasing or possessing 
cigarettes. In recent years, state data on efforts to reduce 
underage smoking demonstrate overwhelmingly that vigorous 
enforcement of legal restrictions on underage access to tobacco 
products will reduce such smoking. 


Dr. Morse will testify that during the last two decades a 
significant shift in thinking has occurred at both the state and 
national level regarding the most effective ways of addressing 
the question of adolescent smoking. Increasingly, states are 
exploring the significant impact that enforcement of laws can 
have on reducing adolescent access to and use of tobacco. 
Since the 1980s it would appear that the many efforts at 
educating the public regarding smoking and health issues have 
had the intended impact of gradually reducing the prevalence 
of adult smokers in the United States. This trend has not been 
mirrored in the smoking prevalence rates of adolescents during 
the same time period. Since the eighties, rates of adolescent 
smoking in general have either stubbornly stayed very stable, 
or within some groupings have actually risen. Data coming 
from both the Monitoring the Future Project and the National 
Youth Risk Behavior Survey underscore these trends in 
adolescent smoking. During this time period at least three 
major studies have found that where communities vigorously 
supported and enforced their laws regarding the prohibition of 
the sale of tobacco to persons under the age of 18 that tobacco 
usage by adolescents dropped significantly. Data from these 
and other studies examining a wide range of issues surrounding 


14] 


the concept of restricting youth access to tobacco by means of 
the enforcement of laws overwhelmingly bolster the argument 
that youth access laws are the most powerful weapon our 
society has to address this health issue. 


Sn AIBASONS ENGAGE IN HIGH-RISK 

Dr. Morse will testify about the reasons shaping a person’s 
tendency to engage in high-risk health behavior. He will testify 
that there are significant others with whom the adolescent 
smoker identifies for his or her self-image and those groups 
consist of peers, family members, extended family members and 
others in the person’s local community. Peer group affiliation 
directly impacts the prevalence of high-risk health behavior 
patterns in adolescents. A person’s specific group or “crowd” 
membership or affiliation exerts a dominant influence on his or 
her high-risk health behavior patterns. Put simply, people like 
to belong to groups and do what their group of peers is doing. 
Adolescents take their cues from their actual circle of peers. 
This fact explains why cigarette advertising has little, if any, 


impact on adolescent smoking behavior. 
PATTERNS 
Dr. Morse will testify that data from multiple sources all 
confirm the power and influence of friends, peers and family on 
why people start to smoke. Analysis of the extensive data 
collected by the Gallup Organization, the Food and Drug 


Administration, the Centers for Disease Control and 
Prevention, and even by defendant’s expert, Dr. Dean 


the factors that adolescents identify consistently. Of equal 
importance is the lack of any survey or poll data that indicate 


iz 142 


that commercial advertising by cigarette companies has any 
impact on an adolescent’s choice to stag smoking. This 
absence of data is especially important when considered within 
the context of the models presented earlier, which stress the 
importance of peers and family members as major sources of 
influence in shaping smoking patterns by adolescents. 


Over the last decade, behavioral science has generated an 
extensive body of research specifically focused on adolescents. 
By far the most significant research on youth health risk 
behavior patterns has been conducted as part of the Youth Risk 
Behavior Surveillance System (YRBSS). The CDC developed 
the YRBSS to monitor health risk behavior patterns of 
adolescents in six major categories: behaviors that contribute 
to unintentional and intentional injuries, tobacco use, alcohol 
and other drug use, sexual behaviors that contribute to 
unintended pregnancy and STDs including HIV infection, 
unhealthy dietary behaviors, and physical inactivity. 


Since data was first collected in 1990, trends analysis has 
reached several significant conclusions. The high-risk health 
behaviors of youth across the six areas of risk behavior are 
highly correlated, as are the factors associated with each risk 
behavior. In other words, young people who engage in any 
one of these behaviors tend to engage in others as well, and the 
set of factors that predict one such behavior also tend to 
predict the others. 


The 1997 YRBSS indicates that many high school age 
youths engage in behavior patterns that may significantly 
contribute to their likelihood of dying. For example, 19.1% 
rarely or never use a safety belt, 35.3% had ridden during the 
30 days preceding the survey with a driver who had been 
drinking alcohol, 32.8% had ever used marijuana, 22.1% had 
carned a weapon during the 30 days preceding the survey, and 
8.6% had attempted suicide during the preceding 12 months. 
In addition, 41.8% of the youth nationwide reported having 
been in a physical fight in the preceding 12 months; of the 50% 


143 


of all high school students who reported being sexually active, 
almost half reported not practicing safe sex techniques. 
Obviously, there is no commercial advertising promoting any 
of these behaviors. 


Dr. Morse will also testify about the study of youth risk 
behavior patterns conducted by the University of Michigan 
Institute for Social Research entitled itori , 
1975-1994. Monitoring the Future has been funded by the 
National Institute of Drug Abuse (NIDA) for the purpose of 
tracking substance use Patterns, onset of use of a wide range 
of substances by adolescents, and their patterns of continued 
use or discontinuation over time. For both legal and illicit 
substances, data are gathered regarding students’ attitudes and 
beliefs, beliefs and behaviors of others in their social 
environment, and _ perceived drug availability in their 
environments. 


The differential rates of various high-risk health behaviors 
generally, and substance use specifically, strongly suggest that 
the observed patterns are best understood as Culturally-based 
or anchored patterns which are shown to be specific to 
particular relatively homogeneous subgroups (e.g. black high 
school seniors), over time and exhibit strong trends suggesting 
substance use tends to occur in cycles and to reflect fads or 
trends that differ within subgroups (e.g. males vs. females, 12 
year olds vs. 16 year olds, etc.). One pattern that seems to be 
very Clear is age cohorts have a tendency to gravitate toward 
adopting those high-risk behavior patterns that preceding 
cohorts did not or do not use. An example of this tendency 
that the reader might easily identify is the recent rise (and 
subsequent decline) in cigar smoking as well as Cigar bars and 
cigar restaurants, trends that appear to have been largely 
unaffected by commercial advertising. 


Researchers are becoming increasingly aware \that as 
individuals move through the life cycle from childhood and 
adolescence into adulthood, the primary socializing agent 


Ee 


144 


responsible for dictating the cultural norms to be followed 
changes dramatically. During childhood, parents and other 
members of an individual’s immediate nuclear, and in some 
cases, extended family, are the most important sources of 
information and the primary socializing agents. This gradually 
changes as a child moves into adolescence. During this next 
phase, the individual is much more attentive to his peers. Not 
until the child reemerges from adolescence and moves into 
young adulthood does he or she anchor his or her patterns of 
socialization based on older adults and adult dominated 
institutions. The general timing of the shifts in the life cycle as 
well as the shifts of the differential influences of peers over any 
particular range of the cultural, material, cognitive and 
normative dimensions of an individual’s socialization are 
determined not only by chronological age, but -social class, 
ethnicity and race as well. 


For all the foregoing reasons and based on my extensive 
professional experience, it is my firm opinion that the decision 
of adolescents to begin smoking is influenced by many social 
and psychological factors, factors which are largely unaffected 
by regulation, and which do not include commercial 
advertising. Accordingly, restricting such advertising will not, 
in my opinion, affect the incidence of underage smoking. In 
particular, in my opinion the regulations challenged in this 
action will not contribute to their stated goal. Since regulation 
will not significantly affect demand, the logical strategy for 
reducing the incidence of underage tobacco is to restrict 
supply. Communities that have successfully reduced underage 
smoking have done so by a concerted and coordinated 
community effort to restrict underage access to tobacco 
products, and not by restricting cigarette advertising. 


/s/ Edward V. Morse 
Edward V. Morse Ph.D.

Expert Report of Lucy L. Henke, Ph.D. 
Professional Background and Qualifications 


1. I am Associate Professor of Marketing at the University 
of Louisiana at Lafayette. An accurate copy of my curriculum 
vitae is attached as Exhibit A to this affidavit. 


2. In addition to my academic activities, while I was 
employed with Louis Harris and Associates, I designed and 
conducted studies of the effectiveness of particular consumer 
advertising campaigns orf behalf of companies engaged in 
consumer brand marketing. After I left Louis Harris, I was 
employed by ABC Television Marketing and Research Services 
Division and had responsibility for tracking viewer perceptions 
of ABC’s and competing networks’ news programming. 


Summary of Opinions 


3. For the reasons summarized in this report, it is my 
opinion, based on my experience in the field of advertising, 
marketing and consumer behavior and my review of the 
pertinent professional literature, that tobacco advertising and 
promotional activity play no role in the decision of minors (or 
anyone else for that matter) to use tobacco products. As a 


EES :“ Spc. 
147 


result, I consider it most unlikely that restricting such activity 
will have any material impact on reducing the incidence of 
underage use of tobacco. In particular, this is true of the 
regulations that have been challenged in this action. 1 will 
discuss, first, generally accepted principles of advertising and 
promotion and consumer behavior that bear on the role of 
commercial marketing in consumer decision making, and, 
second, the application of those principles and the results of an 
extensive body of empirical research on the question of 
whether the commercial advertising and promotion of 
cigarettes and other tobacco products affects the incidence of 
underage tobacco use. A list of the principal publications on 
which I rely for my Opinion is attached. 


Role of Brand Advertising in a Mature Consumer Product 
Market 


4. It is well-established in the field of marketing 
communications that in a mature product market, the effect of 
advertising and promotion is primarily to maintain brand loyalty 
and to encourage consumers of competing brands to switch to 
the advertised brands and to purchase the advertised brand as 
an “alternate” brand if the consumer’s preferred brand is 
unavailable. Let me explain what I mean by a “mature” 
product market. 


5. It has long been recognized that most consumer 
products pass through a product “life cycle.” When a new 
product category is introduced (e.g., DVD players today, 
personal computers nearly twenty years ago), most consumers 
do not know what the product is, what its characteristics are 
and whether the product is useful or desirable. The purpose of 
advertising and promotion at this introductory Stage is to 
stimulate demand for a product category regardless of brand 
As the authors of a recent marketing text write: 


When an organization is the first to introduce an innovative 
product, it tries to stimulate primary demand—demand for 


148 


a product category rather than for a specific brand of 
product—through pioneer promotion. Pioneer promotion 
informs potential customers about the product: what it is, 
what it does, how it can be used, and where it can be 
purchased. Because pioneer promotion is used in the 
introductory stage of the product life cycle, which means 
that there are no competing brands, it neither emphasizes 
brand names nor.compares brands. The first company to 
introduce the compact disc player, for instance, initially 
attempted to stimulate primary demand by emphasizing the 
benefits of compact disc players in general rather than the 
benefit of its specific brand. 


Pride & Ferrell, Marketing: Concepts and Strategies 398 
(1997). For consumers, this type of advertising may be an 
important source of product information because other sources 
of information—e.g., personal experience with the product, the 
experiences of friends and family members, images of product 
use in the media—are largely unavailable as a result of the 
product’s novelty. It is also significant that at this stage, all 
potential consumers, young and old alike, learn about the 
product’s attributes at roughly the same time. 


6. Asaproduct category matures, advertising takes on a 
different role and purpose: from stimulating primary demand 
for the product category to stimulating “selective demand,” i.e. 
maintaining or shifting consumer preferences for particular 
brands. For example, because automobiles are a mature 
product, consumers learn at a very early age from their families 
and their first-hand experience what a car does and for what 
activities a car is useful. People do not need commercia! 
advertisements to help them decide whether they want a car (or 
a VCR or a toothbrush) in the first instance. The market for 
such products has matured and the purpose of advertising in 
such a market is to persuade those who have already decided 
to use the product to select the advertised brand. The focus is 
brand differentiation. For example, one automobile 


149 


manufacturer might stress the roominess and comfortable ride 
ofits products, while another will emphasize sporty handling, 
a third stylish design, and a fourth great value. People who 
have no interest in Owning a car, however, are not a target 
market for the marketers of automobiles, and those people 
largely ignore the vast majority of ads for automobiles. 


7. It is very difficult for a marketer in a mature product 
market to affect aggregate demand for that product category. 
A good recent example of an attempt to do so is the campaign 
sponsored by the dairy industry to increase overall 
consumption of milk. While the “celebrity milk moustache” 
billboards and the “Got milk?” catchphrase are popular, the 
campaign apparently has not resulted in significantly increased 
milk consumption. In fact, consumption has declined every 
year during the time of this campaign. O’Guinn et al (2000); 
Marketing Nev's (1998). This lack of success is not surprising 
in light of what we know about consumer decision making. A 
consumer’s decision whether to drink milk will be affected far 
more by whether the consumer likes the taste of milk and on 
whether family and friends drink milk than on even a very 
clever and engaging advertising campaign. Thus, even where 
a campaign is specifically designed to stimulate demand for a 
product category, it is very difficult through advertising to 
affect aggregate demand for a mature product. A campaign 


designed to promote a specific brand is even less likely to have 
such an effect. 


The Market for Tobacco Products is a Mature Market 


8. Cigarettes and smokeless tobacco products are classic 
examples of mature, if not declining, consumer product 
markets in which advertising is entirely brand advertising that 
seeks to stimulate selective demand for particular brands. In a 
mature or deciining market, sellers are competing for market 
Share and the goals of advertising and promotion are to 
persuade consumers of competing products to switch to the 
advertised brand as their primary or alternate brand choice 


a ae wae ae 


eR 


150 


while, first and foremost, maintaining the brand loyalty of 
existing customers against the efforts of competitors to 
persuade them to switch. In a product market as large as that 
for cigarettes, even small percentage shifts in market share 
have huge economic significance. Even a one percent share of 
such a market represents hundreds of millions of dollars in 
revenues and justifies significant expenditures on advertising 
and promotion to protect and expand one’s market share. 


9. Research in the field’ of marketing confirms that 
tobacco advertising serves to stimulate selective, not primary, 
demand. As Wilcox (Wilcox 1991; Wilcox and Vacker 1992) 
and others have shown, there is no evidence that cigarette 
advertising does anything other than differentiate brands. 
Sturgess (1982) confirms the finding that cigarette advertising, 
which takes place in a mature product category, serves to 
stimulate selective demand. Duffy (1996) investigated the 
effect of total cigarette advertising on aggregate demand for 
cigarettes in the United Kingdom and found no evidence that 
advertising increased total market demand for cigarettes. 
Duffy concluded that a total ban on cigarette advertising would 
not reduce consumption but would have the effect of altering 
the market share of brands or types of tobacco products. The 
conclusions are consistent with the testimony of Daniel Oliver, 
the Chairman of the Federal Trade Commission before the 
Transportation Subcommittee of the House Committee on 
Energy and Commerce on April 3, 1987: “Far from stimulating 
overall demand by conveying new information to consumers 
about the uses of tobacco generally, tobacco advertising is 
likely to have its predominant_impact in affecting the 
consumers’ selection among competing brands.” 


10. The marketing focus of tobacco companies on 
existing smokers is even clearer with regard to promotions 
such as coupons, price discounts, and programs that reward 
brand-loyal smokers with non-tobacco merchandise. It is 
difficult to see how these programs can have any meaningful 


151 


value to non-smokers. A coupon or discount for an undesired 
product is worthless, and even if the premiums offered as a 
reward for repeat purchases are desirable to non-smokers, it is 
hard to believe that a non-smoker will purchase large numbers 
of undesired cigarettes just to earn sufficient credits to obtain 
an item that can be purchased directly for less than the total 
spent on the cigarettes. This is particularly true in light of the 
Master Settlement Agreement in which the major cigarette 
companies agreed not to offer merchandise branded with 
tobacco brand logos even to adult smokers. Whatever appeal 
branded merchandise might have had is obviously no longer 
relevant. 


11. Any suggestion that tobacco products are not a 
mature market because new consumers are entering the market 
and older consumers are leaving it is specious. The same can 
be said of absolutely any consumer product. One of the 
principal characteristics of a mature market is that advertising 
is not a significant initial source of information about the 
product, and this is as true for tobacco products as it is for 
toothpaste. What distinguishes the mature market from 
markets for newly introduced products is that in the latter 
consumers of all ages are learning about the product at the 
same time for the first time without the benefit of prior 
experience. 


12. In short, there is no evidence that the advertising 
or promotion of tobacco products has any effect on the 
initiation of tobacco use, increases aggregate tobacco 
consumption, or does anything other than influence brand 
selection. : 


Effect of Advertising on Behavior of Minors with Regard to 
Tobacco Products. 


13. It is also my opinion that tobacco advertising and 
promotion plays no material role in the decision of minors to 
begin using tobacco products. In addition to the foregoing 


152 


principles, which I believe apply to adolescent consumers as 
well as to adults, I base my opinion on the following points. 


14. First, there is a substantial body of empirical data 
that confirms that the decision of teenagers to use tobacco is 
primarily influenced by peers and family members. This 
research reveals no significant role played by tobacco 
advertising or promotion in influencing that decision. 


15. Second, there is a dearth of credible social science 
research to support the hypothesis that tobacco advertising or 
promotion plays a role in the decision by minors (or anyone 
else for that matter) to begin to use tobacco products. The 
very few studies that purport to support this hypothesis suffer 
from such severe logical and methodological flaws as to 
provide no scientifically valid evidence. 


16. Third, my own research and that of others suggests 
that children are fairly sophisticated consumers who learn at an 
early age that product advertising is intended to persuade and 
that they generally respond skeptically to advertising. 
Awareness of brand logos cannot be equated with a positive 
attitude toward the advertised product or an intent to purchase 
it, and my own research confirms that this is true with regard 
to tobacco advertising. 


17. Fourth, the experience in other countries that have 
imposed restrictions or bans on tobacco advertising suggests 
that such efforts have little or no effect on tobacco 
consumption. 


18. In the remainder of this report, I will elaborate on 
these four points and respond to the arguments made by the 
defendant’s experts. 


Smokers Consistently Fail to Identify Advertising as a Reason 
for the Decision to Begin Smoking 


19. Years of polling research confirms that peer and 
parental influence, and not advertising, drive adolescent 


Ea 


153 


decisions to begin smoking. The Gallup Organization has 
conducted surveys over the years which ask a nationally 
representative sample of adults (1993) and adolescents (1994) 
why they began to smoke. In both surveys the majority of 
respondents identified peer influence as the driving force, 
followed by parental influence. Attached as Exhibits B and C 
are graphs summarizing these results. These surveys were 
open-ended in the sense that Gallup simply asked the question 
without offering a list of possible responses. In a closed-ended 
poll that offered advertising as one of the choices, only 1% of 
the respondents cited advertising as an influence. (Gallup 
1991) Attached as Exhibit D is a graph summarizing these 
results. Consistent with open-ended surveys, peer influence 
was cited by the majority of respondents, followed by parental 
influence, curiosity and rebellion. Social Science research using 
survey methodology which permits respondents to select 
multiple influences yields the same results. (Zinser 1997) 
Attached as Exhibit E is a graph suminarizing these results. 


20. Contrary to the contentions of some that 
individuals may be reluctant to identify advertising as an 
influence due to embarrassment, the Zinser poll confirmed that 
advertising is an influence on brand choice. Specifically, when 
respondents were asked about the influence that advertising 
had on brand choice, advertising came in third out of five 
_ Research in other areas confirms that people are willing to 
identify advertising as an influence on decisionmaking when it zi 
does, in fact, have such an influence. 


Source Other Than A dvertising and Promotion Significantly 
Influence Underage Tobacco Use 


21. Many studies provide evidence that young people 
acquire information about products, including tobacco 
products, from many sources other than advertising. (Adler, et 
al., 1980; Tinsley 1992; Moschis 1985; Ward, Wackman and 
Wartella 1977) 


154 


** * * 


30. Numerous other studies corroborate and confirm 
the conclusion that relatives and peers are the most significant 
influences on a minor’s decision to begin smoking. They 
include: Oygard et al. (1995), Wang et al. (1997), Jackson 
(1997), Conrad et al. (1992), McDonald (1993), “Teenage 
Smoking: National Patterns of Cigarette Smoking, Ages 12 
through 18, in 1968 and 1970,” U.S. Dept. of Health, 
Education and Welfare (1970); Gallup polls (1977, 1994); 
Zinser (1994). 


¢« Absence of Credible Evidence Linking Tobacco 
Advertising with Smoking Initiation 


31. Second, there is no credible social science research 
that supports the proposition that exposure to tobacco 
advertising leaders to smoking initiation, and no evidence 
whatsoever that the particular marketing practices prohibited 
in the regulations challenged in the litigation have any impact 
on the decision >f adolescents to begin to use tobacco. ( 


32. In order to demonstrate a link between tobacco 
advertising and underage smoking initiation, it would be 
necessary to undertake a “longitudinal study,” i.e. a valid study 
of a sample of minors over time that measured changes in 
smoking behavior and found a statistically significant 
relationship be:ween exposure to tobacco advertising or 
promotion and such changes in behavior. As noted above, 
there are a nunber of longitudinal studies demonstrating the 
influence of family and peers on smoking inititiation. There is\ 
only one published paper that claims to have found such a 
relationship between tobacco marketing and smoking initiation: 
Pierce et al. (1998). The Pierce study claims to be the first 
longitudinal study to “provide[] clear evidence that tobacco 
industry advertsing and promotional activities can influence 
nonsusceptible never smokers to start the process of becoming 
addicted to cigarettes.” (Pierce 1998, at 514) It does nothing 


\ 


155 


of the sort. For the reasons set forth below, the Pierce study 
is deeply flawed and provides no scientifically valid evidence 
for any link between tobacco marketing and smoking initiation 
by minors. 


** * * 


48. The only other longitudinal study that I have seen 
which purports to find a link between tobacco advertising and 
promotion and smoking initiation is an unpublished article by 
Biener and Siegel (in press) that was submitted to the court in 
this case. This article employs essentially the same 
methodology as the Pierce study, and consequently suffers 
from the same deficiencies. Indeed, the authors corroborate 
my opinion that “receptivity” is a proxy for the influence of 
family and friends rather than an independent predictor. They 
state: “Receptivity was significantly associated with living in 
a household where at least one aduit.smoked, having one or 
more smokers among one’s close friends, being an early 
experimenter and scoring above the median in rebelliousness.” 


49. I am aware of nothing else that even purports to be 
a longitudinal study demonstrating any kind of causal link 
between tobacco advertising or promotion and smoking 
initiation by minors (or anyone else for that matter). Since the 
Pierce and Biener studies provide no credible evidence to 
support such a link and since the literature is replete with 
studies identifying other factors such as peer and parental 
influence as of primary importance, I conclude that there is no 
basis in social science literature to conclude that such a causal 
link exists. The absence of such evidence is not surprising 
since it would contradict what students of marketing have long 
understood about the role of advertising and promotion in a 
mature product market. 


~ 


156 


te 


Children are Relatively Sophisticated Consumers and 
Typically Learn to React Skeptically to Advertising. 


50. The arguments in favor of advertising bans are 
premised on the assumption that minors are vulnerable 
“sponges,” incapable of recognizing the persuasive intent of 
advertising messages. However, this assumption has been 
thoroughly discredited by studies of children’s perceptions of 
advertising. 


* * * * 


54. The later studies demonstrated that when a 
nonverbal measure is used, even younger children can express 
an understanding of the persuasive intent of ads. Studies by 
Henke (1980) and Donohue, Henke and Donohue (1980) 
found that as many as 75% of 3-year-olds were able to express 
an understading of the persuasive intent of ads when a 
nonverbal measure was used. By the age of 6, 96% of the 
children in these studies indicated an understanding of selling 
intent when a non-verbal measure was used. 


=. The significance of this research is that children, 
like adults, who understand the persuasive intent of an 
advertisement are more likely to be skeptical, even cynical, 
about the advertised message. The concept of “source 
credibility’ helps to explain the relationship between 
identification of the selling intent of an ad and skepticism of the 
advertised message. 


** * * 


75. In fact, a study I conducted with Dr. Michelle Wolf 
(Henke and Wolf 1995) demonstrated that parents of young 
children have used the Joe Camel brand-advertising logo to 
reinforce anti-smoking messages with their children. Parents 
as wel! as older siblings and peers play a very important role in 
influencing young people’s decisions about smoking. Using 
cigarette advertising symbols to deliver anti-smoking messages 


157 


to children is but one example of the way in which consumers 
use advertising. 


Cigarette Advertising is not “Pervasive” to Minors 


76. Advocates of tobacco advertising bans frequently 
claim that extreme steps are needed because tobacco 
advertising is “pervasive.” This term is not usually defined, but 
the rhetoric of “pervasiveness” is usually coupled with figures 
(derived from FTC reports) that suggest that the tobacco 
industry spends vast sums on advertising and promotion. 


77. A comparison between the tobacco industry and 
other consumer product industries, however, reveals that 
tobacco marketing expenditures are comparatively modest. 
For example, attached as Exhibit F is a chart (based on 
Competitive Media LNA/Media Watch Multi-Media Service 
Ad Dollar Summary, January-December 1996) showing where 
tobacco advertising expenditures compare with advertising 
expenditures for other industries. Amounts spent on 
advertising tobacco products in the United States is less than 
10% of the amounts spent on marketing automobiles, and 
considerably less than the amounts spent on marketing food, 
toiletries and cosmetics,-computers/office equipment, apparel, 
insurance, and many other product categories. In 1997, the 
amount spent on cigarette advertising was only .8% of the total 
amount spend on advertising in the U.S. See Exhibit G 


— 


78. Moreover, tobacco marketing is completely absent 
in broadcast media. It is well-known that television is the 
medium most likely to reach underage audiences. In addition, 
the 1998 settlement agreement between tobacco manufacturers 
and 46 states including Massachusetts imposes further limits on 
cigarette advertising (e.g. no transit advertising, no billboards). 
To be sure, the research summarized above makes it clear that 
minors are familiar with at least the most widely promoted 
cigarette brands. It is, however, misleading to Say that 


158 


cigarette advertising today is a dominant presence in the 
advertising that young people encounter 


There is No Discernible Relationship between Expenditures on 
Cigarette Advertising and Promotion and the Incidence of 
/nderage Smoking. 


79 Some advocates of restrictions on tobacco 
advertising and promotion have claimed to have found 
relationships between specific cigarette advertising campaigns 
and trends in smoking incidence. The publicly available data 
simply do not support this contention. 


80. Attached as Exhibit H to this report is a graph that 
plots cigarette sales and tobacco industry marketing 
expenditures (all as reflected on industry reports to the Federal 
Trade Commission) for the period 1970-1995. Cigarette sales 
have generally declined while advertising expenditures either 
rose or remained constant while spending on non-advertising 
promotions generally increased. Nothing in these data suggests 
any causal link between marketing expenditures and smoking 
consumption. 


81. Moreover, if one compares aggregate spending on 
cigarette advertising and promotion and the incidence of youth 
smoking over time, it is also apparent that there is no 
relationship. Attached as Exhibit I is a graph that plots tobacco 
industry marketing expenditures (as reflected on industry 
reports to the FTC) and the percentage of high school seniors 
smoking in the past 30 days (derived from the University of 
Michigan’s “Monitoring the Future Survey”). As the graph 
illustrates, there is no basis for inferring a causal link between 
marketing expenditures and adolescent smoking. The 
incidence of youth smoking has generally declined but even in 
years when it has not declined there is no evident relationship 
between tobacco marketing expenditures in the period 
immediately preceding the change and the change itself. 


** * * 


159 


86 Advocates of advertising restrictions also argue 
that adolescents are encouraged to begin smoking through the 
“allure” of toacco advertising which conveys images of 
“independence, healthfulness, adventure-seeking and youthful 
activities.” They further argue that teens take up smoking to 
achieve these desirable qualities. In fact, however, there is 
almost no consumer product being advertised today that does 
not utilize one or more of these very same themes and images 
in its advertising Truth be told, the old, the infirm, the 
dependent and the “boring” are not frequently (if ever) pictured 
in advertising for any consumer product. In light of the 
ubiquity of the themes of youth, attractiveness, health, and 
adventure in American advertising today, one must ask why a 
teen would choose to smoke to identify with these attributes 
rather than simply buy an advertised fragrance, show or blue 
jean unless something more than advertising was in play. 


Experience in Countries that have Restricted or Banned 
Tobacco Advertising Suggests that such Actions have Little 
Effect on Smoking Rates 


87. If the advocates of restrictions on tobacco 
advertising and promotion were correct, then one would expect 
to see that the implementation of such restrictions in other 
countries would consistently result in a reduction of smoking 
incidence, particularly on the part of minors. In fact, the data 
do not support this conclusion. The experience of countries 
that have implemented advertising bans is far more equivocal. 
Indeed, some of the countries that have implemented such bans 
have experienced an actual increase in smoking rates. 


88. In Canada, when cigarette brand advertising was 
banned in 1990, consumption among young people went up. 
This increase occurred after a twenty year decline in overall 
consumption in Canada. In Norway, consumption of tobacco 
in 1996 remained unchanged from 1975, the year an 
advertising ban was introduced. In Finland, smoking among 
young people has increased since the advertising ban of 1978. 


160 


89 In addition, it is worth noting (as the Surgeon 
General did in his 1979 report) that in Communist countries 
such as China and the former Soviet Union, where consumer 
advertising was not permitted, smoking rates were quite high 
Rosch (1996) reported that “[w]hile smoking decreased by 25- 
38% in Western Europe during the last decade, it has increased 
4-11% annually in Russia” He further notes that more than 
15% of Sth grade Russian boys smoke. In China, tobacco use 
rates are increasing Li, et al. (1996) report that smoking is 
prevalent among early adolescent Chinese youth, and that the 
annual increase of cigarette consumption between 1982 and 
1990 was over 7% (referencing Stanley, 1993).

LAURENCE J. DeFRANCO, being of legal age and duly 
sworn, deposes and say: 


1. I am the president and co-founder of InContext, Inc., 
an international information company located in Washington, 
D.C. that performs digital computer mapping and charting of 
economic data. I make this affidavit based on personal 
knowledge. 


2. I have expertise in the field of geo-economics, which 
merges the disciplines of economics, geography, and computer 
science. More detailed information about my background, 


163 


including the articles and books I have authored, and 
information about InContext, is attached hereto as Exhibit A. 


3. In May of 1998 I was asked to prepare, and did prepare 
on behalf of the four tobacco companies that are plaintiffs in 
these consolidated actions, maps depicting the precise amount 
of area which would remain potentially available to outdoor 
advertisers of tobacco products in three Massachusetts cities 
under regulations proposed by the Massachusetts Attorney 
General that would restrict outdoor tobacco advertising. 
Proposed 940 CMR 2.04(d). The maps that I prepared, and an 
accurate description of the methodology I used to prepare the 
maps, were filed in the administrative record on Proposed 940 
CMR § 2.01 et S€q., as attachments to Comments on the 
proposed regulations filed by the four companies dated June 
18, 1998. True and correct copies of the maps I prepared, and 
of the statement of my methodology, are attached as Exhibits 
B and C hereto. 


4. The maps I prepared show the area that would remain 
available for outdoor advertisers of tobacco products if the 
Attomey General’s prohibition on outdoor advertising covered 
an area within a radius of 1,000 yards from schools, parks and 
playgrounds (as indicated in the Attorney General’s Proposed 
Regulations), and also showed the area that would remain 
available for outdoor advertising if the prohibition covered an 
area within a radius of 1,000 feet, from schools, parks and 
playgrounds, which is the area of the prohibition indicated in 
the Attorney General’s final regulations, now codified at 940 
CMR § 21.01 et seq. 


5. As described in the attached statement of the 
methodology I used to prepare the maps, Exhibit C hereto, the 
attached mapping analysis involves no assumptions, no theories 
and no estimates. It is based on official United States 
Geological Survey maps for each City. 


164 


6. My analysis demonstrated that, if a 1,000 foot radius is 
used to define the area where outdoor advertising is prohibited, 
which is the area specified in Section 21.05(5) of the Attorney 
General’s final regulations: (a) the percentage of the Land Area 
of the City of Boston that would be unavailable for outdoor 
tobacco advertising is 90.6%; (b) the percentage of the Land 
Area of the City of Worcester that would be unavailable for 
outdoor tobacco advertising is 87.8%; and (c) the percentage 
of the Land Area of the City of Springfield that would be 
unavailable for outdoor tobacco advertising is 88.8%. See 
Exhibit C hereto. These results are depicted on the maps 
attached hereto as Exhibit B.

Rolf Provan, being duly sworn, deposes and states as 
follows: 


1. I am director of trade development at Brown & 
Williamson Tobacco Corporation (“B&W”) and have held this 
position since February 1998. Prior to becoming Director of 
Trade Development, I was Director of Marketing Information 
from April 1996 through January 1998; Brand Manager from 
September 1995 to March 1996; Regional Director from 
February 1994 through August 1995; Area Market Manager 
from July 1992 through January 1994; International Marketing 
Coordinator from December 1991 to July 1992. From 1989 to 
1991, I worked in various local sales and marketing positions 
with affiliates of B& W located in Chile and Hong Kong. I am 
competent to make this affidavit, which is based on my 
personal knowledge of the information stated below. 


2. During my nearly eight years at B&W, I have become 
knowledgeable about B&W’s cigarette sales and marketing 
programs to retail outlets. Over the past 18 months, I have 
worked closely on the development and implementation of 
B&W cigarette promotional and merchandising programs. As 
part of my job responsibilities, | have become very familiar with 
B&W’s present marketing efforts for all of its major domestic 
cigarette brands, including the impact that promotional 


170 


programs, including buy-downs, stickers and other discounting 
methods, and merchandising programs, such as indoor displays 
and signage, have on the sale of cigarette brands. 


3. B&W is the third largest cigarette manufacturer in the 
United States. Last year, B&W’s share of the domestic market 
was approximately 15%. B&W manufactures and sells both 
“premium” brands, such as Kool®, Carlton®, Capri® and 
Lucky Stnke®, and value-for-money, or “value” brands, such 
as GPC®. 


4. The purpose of B&W’s marketing efforts is to increase 
our share of adult smokers by building and developing brand 
equity (the reputation and good will associated with a 
particular brand) for our cigarettes. Increasing brand equity 
maintains loyalty among current adult smokers of B&W brands 
and encourages adult smokers of competitive brands of 
cigarettes to switch to a B&W brand. 


5. Brown & Williamson’s marketing plans are noi 
designed or intended to attract non-smokers, or smokers under 
the age of 21. Since the mid-1960’s, B&W has adhered to 
internal and industry cigarette marketing codes designed to 
restrict our intended advertising audience to adults 21 years or 
older, and limit our marketing to techniques, locations and 
mediums aimed at adult smokers. B&W has supported 
industry efforts and initiated company efforts to educate minors 
not to smoke and to educate retailers not to sell to minors. 
Our Corporate and Youth Responsibility department works 
companywide to monitor our marketing efforts and to develop 
new strategies and programs to reduce underage smoking. 


6. I understand that the Challenged Regulations would 
prohibit self-service product displays and require all tobacco 
products to be kept out of the reach of consumers at all 
retailers, and would eliminate outdoor advertising and indoor 
advertising lower than five feet off the ground at many retail 
outlets. 


17] 


7. Our experience at B&W indicates that the retail 
environment is the primary source of brand awareness for adult 
smokers. 


8. Channels for communicating with adult consumers 
regarding cigarettes have been drastically reduced due to the 
current regulatory environment. Because many traditional 
avenues of communication, including but not limited to 
outdoor billboards, transit stop and taxi signs, have been 
eliminated, retail display space and signage is more crucial now 
than ever before to the advertising and promotion of cigarette 
brands. 


9. Laws that restrict or eliminate self-service displays, 
such as 940 CMR §§ 21.04(2)(c) & (d) are causing available 
retail display space for cigarettes to shrink to the limited space 
available behind the sales counter (the “backbar”’) or beneath 
the counter and wholly out of sight.. This is especially true in 
“pack outlets,” e.g., convenience and gas stores, where 
available display area is already much less than in supermarkets 
and other types of stores. This limited space is usually devoted 
to only a few brands. 


10. Similarly, laws that restrict or eliminate retail signage, 
both indoor and outdoor, further cause a dramatic reduction in 
the avenues available to B&W to communicate to adult 
consumers who choose to smoke. 


11. The display of a cigarette brand at retail is a vitally 
important form of communication which gives the 
manufacturer the opportunity to reinforce the brand’s existence 
and prominence to smokers of that brand and also provides the 
primary opportunity to encourage adult smokers to pick up, 
view and purchase a pack of an unfamiliar brand or a newly 
introduced or repositioned brand. : 


12. If a cigarette brand is not visible at retail or is relegated 
to space that is not in the line of sight of a potential adult 
consumer, that brand will have great difficulty communicating 


172 


its availability and/or competitive characteristics as compared 
with more visibly displayed brands. 


13. Space for both product displays and promotional 
materials at pack outlets, as well as all outlets which sell 
tobacco products, is significantly limited. Much of the 
available space for visible display and promotion at the point- 
of-sale at pack outlets are self service displays on countertops. 


14. In addition, the requirement that all advertising be 
placed five feet or more above the ground at most retail outlets 
further eliminates alternative means that B&W uses to 
communicate with adult customers, such as change mats, cash 
register signs, counter top signs, or mid-level display rack 
signs. Common sense and experience indicates that the amount 
of space five feet or more above the floor that is not taken up 
by shelving, coolers or freezers, windows, and is otherwise 
available for advertising is limited, especially at pack outlets. 
Even now, retailers generally are paid a premium for such 
space. 


15. Point of sale advertising and merchandising, including 
primarily self-service displays, are critical to B&W’s 
communication with its current customers as well as potential 
customers who currently smoke non-B&W brands. Indeed, 
many of our key brands are marketed predominantly through 
the use of self service displays and by promoting price 
competition at the retail counter. 


16. B&W’s value for money brands are promoted almost 
exclusively through self-service displays and other poiri-of sale 
advertising. The success of GPC® and other value brands is 
dependent on their ability to attract customers of premium 
brands at the point of sale through price promotions, including 
use of discount stickers on packs, buy-one-get-one-free 
programs, and “buy downs” in which B&W sales 
representatives pay a retailer to discount the consumer price on 
a given brand. The ability to communicate these price 


173 


promotions to adult smokers at the point-of-sale through ready 
access to marked-down packs at countertop product displays 
are therefore central to the competitive existence of these 
brands. The Massachusetts regulations would render these 
products virtually invisible, denying adult consumers even basic 
identifying information such as what the pack label looks like. 


17.The growth of the value segment has come 
predominately from consumers who, upon seeing a self-service 
display and other in-store promotions, decide to try a new 
B&W value-for-money cigarette rather than their usual 
premium brand cigarette. Unless an adult consumer can see 
the value cigarette and the price at the point of sale, our 
experience teaches us that loyal customers may believe that the 
retail outlet does not carry the product and that potential new 
customers will never learn of the product or the promotion. 


18. Historically, we have observed that consumers have 
imperfect pricing and promotional information about the value 
brands. If B&W is not able to indicate to the consumer at the 
point of sale pricing and other information, consumers will 
simply purchase visibly-displayed premium brand cigarettes 
with which they are more familiar. 


19. With little or no space to display, advertise and 
promote, it will be extremely difficult for B&W to 
communicate with consumers regarding B&W’s premium 
brands as well. The ban on self-service display and the 
significant restrictions on other point-of-sale advertising make 
it more difficult, if not impossible, to reposition the image or 
significantly raise adult consumer awareness of an existing 
brand. Over the past few years, B&W has invested large sums 
in repositioning many of our premium brands, such as Kook® 
and Carlton®. Self-service display was of vital importance for 
B&W to communicate to current brand smokers and potential 
brand-switchers the product’s new brand identity and 
packaging. 


174 


20. Further, B&W’s launch of new brand styles, most 
recently Lucky Strike Filters® and Kool Natural®, are heavily 
dependent on point-of-sale promotion and self-service display. 
Self-service displays allow an adult consumer to examine a new 
product and review its packaging, which may contain brand 
equity, price or other product information, customer service 
contacts, and, of course, the Surgeon General’s warning. 


21. As the third-ranking cigarette manufacturer, it is 
especially important for B&W to achieve visibility for its 
brands at retail in order to persuade adult smokers to continue 
to smoke or to switch to our brands. It is our experience that 
adult consumers will be much more likely to purchase our 
brands if we are able to sufficiently communicate our brands, 
and price promotions to customers through self-service 
displays and point-of-sale advertising. 


22. If our products are forced behind or under the counter 
throughout the Commonwealth of Massachusetts by these 
regulations, B&W will be denied critical avenues of speech to 
lawful consumers of our products. If, as the regulations 
contemplate, all tobacco products are forced behind the 
counter, the less dominant brands, value brands, niche 
products, and new products will become virtually invisible. 
The five-foot restriction on indoor that would be placed on 
many retail establishments will further eliminate B&W’ s ability 
to communicate with adult smokers. Due to our market 
position and the array of brands B&W sells, most if not all 
B&W brands will be stored out of view and reach of 
customers. Being denied these avenues of communication 
would cause B& W irreparable injury.
